#157. ‘Windrush’ accusations over sharp rise in Turkish nationals facing removal from UK

David Lammy among those raising concerns after errors found in rejected applications for indefinite leave to remain

Concerns have been raised over the sudden rise in number of Turkish nationals facing removal from the UK as a result of mistakes, prompting accusations that the Home Office has failed to learn the lessons of the Windrush scandal.

Since the start of the year, there has been a sharp rise in the number of rejected applications for indefinite leave to remain for those on European Community Association Agreement (ECAA) visas, a longstanding route to residency for Turkish nationals seeking to set up a business in the UK.

The rise in refusals appears to apply to applications lodged since September 2025, shortly before Shabana Mahmood announced controversial proposals at the Labour conference for tougher conditions on those applying for leave to remain.

The home secretary said at the time: “In solving this crisis, you may not always like what I do. We will have to question some of the assumptions and legal constraints that have lasted for a generation and more.”

The Home Office maintains that there has been no change to the rules but doubts about this have been raised, including by the former foreign secretary David Lammy, who said there “seems to be a sudden change” in the government’s policy, which had “devastated” the lives of those affected and evoked memories of the Windrush scandal.

Hundreds of ECAA holders have been refused permanent residency since the start of the year, a big uptick compared with previous years, while the number of successful applications has plummeted from the usual several thousand to just a few hundred since January.

Refusal letters have been riddled with basic errors, made-up requirements and a disregard of human tragedies, which were previously treated with discretion.

Many of those being denied arrived in the UK within the past seven years and have set up successful businesses. Among them is a woman who started treatment for cancer in Turkey after being diagnosed on a trip during the Covid-19 pandemic, when the NHS restricted such care. Her application was denied because she “chose” to begin treatment in Turkey, even though her medical team advised that transferring clinical care treatment “carried significant clinical risk”.

Despite a review, the Home Office stood firm and informed the woman in October that she and her three young children “should now leave the United Kingdom”.

Another refused applicant was told his window-cleaning business was not legitimate because he did not have a social media presence, a requirement that does not exist. One woman whose father in Turkey lives with terminal Alzheimer’s was denied indefinite leave to remain because the Home Office said his condition had been “known for several years”, meaning her absences from the UK did not stem from an “urgent unforeseen situation”.

Lammy, the MP for Tottenham, a constituency with a large Turkish-speaking population, said: “I have been contacted by constituents whose lives have been devastated by what seems to be a sudden change in Home Office policy – people who have built their lives and families here and played by the rules.

“I can’t help remembering the Windrush scandal when I hear these terrible stories. The Home Office needs to urgently revisit and consider the Lessons Learned review. This is entirely unacceptable. I would like to see justice for Britain’s Turkish-origin community.”

The number of ECAA applications under administrative review, a process where the Home Office re-examines a refusal, has soared since the start of the year, according to a freedom of information request, jumping from 145 in 2025 to 395 from January to mid-August 2026. Lawyers dealing with ECAA cases have told the Guardian there have been long delays for applications lodged from September 2025, beyond the usual six-month wait time.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Data from the department shows a stark fall in the number of Turkish nationals applying through this route being granted indefinite leave to remain. From January to June 2025, there were 3,378 grants of settlement in the UK for ECAA applicants. For the same period in 2026, this plummeted to 745, a 78% drop.

Lawyers have said these types of cases were previously routinely approved because of guidance on compassionate circumstances but that a harsher shift occurred for applications lodged from last September.

Home Office caseworkers also appear to be making significant errors or applying requirements that do not exist. In one refusal, which was eventually overturned, the department said it had been “unable to locate a website for your business” or any “evidence of any online presence, for example social media presence” and falsely claimed the applicant did not provide a complete set of bank statements. In another, the Home Office wrongly calculated the number of days the applicant was out of the country, incorrectly claiming they were out of the country for more than 180 days within a 12-month period.

The Home Office’s approach to ECAA holders appears to contradict certain recommendations made in the independent review into the Windrush scandal, which Priti Patel, the home secretary at the time, accepted and promised to act on. These included establishing clearer guidance on the burden and standard of proof, increasing direct contact with applicants, and revising the criteria and process for assessing cases involving vulnerable applicants.

A Home Office spokesperson said: “There has been no change to how ECAA settlement applications are considered. Every application continues to be considered on its individual circumstances, in line with the published rules and guidance.”

#154. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#151. Reintroduction of white-tailed eagles puts seabird colonies at risk of wipeout, say experts

Naturalists divided over potential impact of proposals to increase number of wild raptors in England and Wales

White-tailed eagles reintroduced into England and Wales imperil endangered seabird populations and risk wiping out some colonies, ecologists and conservationists are warning.

Puffins, guillemots, razorbills, fulmars and kittiwakes are among the birds for which breeding populations could collapse if large numbers of white-tailed eagles are released, according to some academics and conservationists.

“Amidst all the excitement and enthusiasm for reintroducing white-tailed eagles, the fact that these birds will probably destroy our seabird colonies seems to have been completely overlooked,” said Tim Birkhead, an emeritus professor of ecology and evolution at the University of Sheffield.

The return of white-tailed eagles to Britain after they were driven to extinction in 1918 has been a great conservation success story in recent decades. But proposals to release up to 60 eagles in Gwent, south Wales, on top of 20 young birds already being released over the next three years on Exmoor, is dividing nature lovers.

The Gwent Wildlife Trust is supporting the proposed Gwent reintroduction while the Wildlife Trust of South & West Wales (WTSWW) is opposed.

Last month an eagle from the Exmoor release was sighted over the south Wales island of Skomer, a renowned seabird sanctuary where David Attenborough was filmed encountering puffins and shearwaters for his Wild Isles series. British seabirds have suffered the worst declines of any group of birds over the last five years, with populations falling by a fifth, but the seabird populations in south Wales are relatively healthy.

Sarah Kessell, the chief executive of WTSWW, said: “We’re increasingly alarmed about the reintroduction of white-tailed eagles across the UK as more evidence comes to light of their impact on cliff-nesting seabirds.

“The islands of Skomer and Skokholm are a sanctuary for globally important seabird populations, which are already under threat. The extra pressure of disturbance and displacement from white-tailed eagles could have a significant impact on breeding seabirds. We have an overwhelming obligation to protect the seabirds on the islands that we manage.”

White-tailed eagle chicks brought over from Norway to reintroduce them into Scotland in 1975 has led to a wild population now of 200 breeding pairs, with 45 birds successfully reintroduced to the Isle of Wight since 2019. Although there has been repeated illegal killing of the huge avian predator, the first English-born wild bird fledged from a nest in East Sussex in 2023. Wales has not yet restored the species to its skies.

Prof Geoff Hilton, the head of wetland evidence at the Wildfowl and Wetlands Trust (WWT), which is leading the Gwent reintroduction, said: “Conservation action has and always will carry an element of risk. But what’s essential is that we carefully evaluate those risks early, use robust evidence to investigate the scale of those risks, and balance them against potential opportunities.”

Hilton emphasised that white-tailed eagles were generalist feeders with a varied diet including carrion, fish and small mammals. Research published in 2020 of eagles east of the Baltic Sea found that birds of particular conservation concern in the EU accounted for approximately 1% of identified prey in a long-term study of eagle diets.

He said: “With careful phasing, we aim to establish seven to nine breeding pairs of white-tailed eagles over the next 10 years. Therefore, we don’t expect this reintroduction to significantly reduce or impact any single species population. We do, however, expect to see healthier, more balanced ecosystems and landscapes, as apex predators like these eagles start to thrive once again.”

In evidence submitted by WTSWW against the Gwent proposals, Stephen Votier, a professor of seabird conservation and ecology at Heriot-Watt University, said: “It is clear that white-tailed eagles can have severe impacts on seabird populations – they seem especially vulnerable because the eagle can indirectly affect social aspects of breeding.”

As well as directly predating seabirds and their young, indirect effects are likely to be much greater, say some researchers, because the fear induced by white-tailed eagles causes adult seabirds to temporarily desert their breeding sites, exposing chicks to predation by other birds including gulls and corvids.

Birkhead, who has studied the guillemot population on Skomer since 1972, said he would “love to see white-tailed eagles” but said scientific evidence of the predators hastening the extinction of seabird colonies in Scandinavia must be properly considered.

In 2023, a scientific paper found that in northern Norway an increasing abundance of white-tailed eagles accelerated the loss of a kittiwake colony in Røst in 2020 by “many years”. The colony was already in decline but the eagles’ impacts were greater because fewer fish for the kittiwakes compelled the birds to spend more time foraging and less time defending their young from predators.

Annette Fayet and Tycho Anker-Nilssen, co-authors of the study, said that although Røst had only two or three breeding pairs of eagles, large numbers of immature eagles gathered there and disturbed the nesting seabirds.

Asked about the applicability of their findings to Wales, they said: “We cannot assume that the situation in Norway will be reproduced in south Wales but find it impossible to rule out that something similar could happen there too, in time.”

Roger Morgan-Grenville, the author of a book about white-tailed eagles that will be published next spring, said there was a consistent association between the rising number of white-tailed eagles on the Baltic coast and the reduced breeding success of the cormorant, and similar negative impacts on northern gannets and kittiwakes in western Norway. He said: “I’m absolutely in favour of these magnificent birds covering our skies again but we need to listen to all voices, and not just the loud ones, and we need to accept that the landscape that the white-tailed has come back into is a far more depleted one than it left in 1918.”

Natural Resources Wales is assessing the application for the release of white-tailed eagles in Gwent, with a verdict expected by the end of this year. A spokesperson said: “If we are minded to grant a licence, there will be a public consultation process from late 2026 into early 2027 before any final decision is made.”

#149. Briton among six dead after gunman opens fire in Kraków market square

Three more people found dead at home of suspect, who minister says held ‘ultra-nationalistic’ views

Six people were killed and 16 wounded after a gunman opened fire in a busy market square in Kraków, authorities said, in what Poland’s defence minister called an “act of terror” on the people of the city.

The suspect, a 25-year-old Polish citizen, opened fire with an automatic weapon in the city’s main square at about 3pm on Saturday before being apprehended by police.

He was fascinated with far-right ideologies and held “very radical” and “ultra-nationalistic” views, the Polish interior minister, Marcin Kierwiński, said at a news conference.

Four women and two men were killed, one of them a British citizen, Polish police said. Three of the victims were Polish women and the remaining two victims had not been identified.

Five foreign nationals from Britain, Austria, Germany, Portugal and Russia were wounded, a Polish government spokesperson said.

Hours after the attack, the prime minister, Donald Tusk, said authorities found the bodies of three of the suspect’s close family members – two women and a man – inside his home in Ostrowiec Świętokrzyski, a city about 90 miles (140km) north-east of Kraków.

The ⁠injured also included two police ⁠officers, one of whom was in a serious condition after being shot three times in the exchange of fire with the alleged attacker.

A total of 13 people were taken to hospital in serious conditions, authorities said. That figure may include the gunman, who underwent surgery after the attack.

It was unclear how many of the injured were shot. Two of them were treated and released within hours of the attack.

Jonathan Balshaw, from Ipswich in England, was outside St Mary’s basilica in the square with his girlfriend when the attack began. “We ran down the side of the church and through the side door and hid with several other people until the gunfire died down,” he told the BBC.

“A lot of shots were fired, more than 20, with it sounding like multiple different firearms – there were two distinctly different noises.”

Videos posted on social media by witnesses showed people running away from the square to take cover on small streets nearby, as well as first responders trying to resuscitate victims.

As it emerged that a British citizen was among the dead, the UK prime minister, Andy Burnham, paid tribute to the victims of the attack. “I’m shocked and saddened by the appalling shooting in Kraków today,” he said. “My thoughts are with the families of those who have lost their lives, everyone injured and all those affected by this senseless violence.”

A tram driver reported at about 3pm that his vehicle had been shot at by a man in a moving car while passing near the main square, the Kraków municipal transport company said. The window to the tram driver’s cabin was shattered.

The attacker wore military-style fatigues and a bulletproof vest, the Kraków police spokesperson Katarzyna Cisło said. Police found other weapons and ammunition in his car. He kept firing at the police even after being shot himself, she said.

The attacker engaged in a “wild ride” around the main square, which is why people were wounded at several locations, Cisło said, adding that the gunman appeared to have acted alone.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

The Polish defence minister, Władysław Kosiniak-Kamysz, called the shooting an “act of terror” against the people of Kraków, Poland’s second-largest city, which receives 15 million visitors a year, most of them passing through the main square.

The mayor of Kraków, Łukasz Gibała, said the city would be in official mourning until Wednesday. He vowed that Kraków would remain open to visitors despite the tragedy.

“The square will belong to us again,” Gibała said in a video posted on X.

On Saturday evening, the government spokesperson Adam Szłapka wrote on X: “Today’s event in Kraków has shaken all of Poland,” adding that Tusk was “on his way to Kraków”.

The justice minister, Waldemar Żurek, wrote on X that authorities would “ensure that the perpetrator of this crime is held fully accountable and severely punished”.

The mayor of Warsaw, Rafał Trzaskowski, announced increased security measures, including road barriers, along main streets in the Polish capital’s old town.

Earlier this year, Euronews reported that the number of legally owned firearms in Poland exceeded 1m for the first time.

Poland issued a record 50,700 gun permits in 2025 and the number of active permits registered in 2025 was almost double the number registered in 2015, according to the police statistics cited in the report.

Sunday and Monday will be a period of national mourning for the victims of the attack.

#148. France sees new round of Yellow Vest, student protests

Although fewer protesters turned out in comparison with previous weeks, several cities saw clashes between police and demonstrators.

Fewer protesters than expected turned out for the fourth weekend of mass demonstrations in France, although clashes with police were reported in several cities, the Interior Ministry said on Saturday.

Authorities had expected up to 170,000 people to protest nationwide, including 60,000 in Paris.

The ministry reported a slightly lower nationwide total of 146,000 people, out of which some 46,000 took to the streets of the French capital, where the afternoon was reported to have remained mostly calm, with some isolated incidents reported in the evening involving the deployment of tear gas.

Protest organizers gave a higher nationwide count of over 250,000 protesters, which is nevertheless fewer than the 320,000 to 500,000 that were reported to have turned out last Saturday.

Clashes between police and protesters were reported in Toulouse, Marseille, Lyon and Bordeaux.

The Interior Ministry reported some 350 arrests nationwide by the evening in connection with the protests.

Students and labor unions had called an authorized central Paris march "against the high cost of living and in support of young people."

In Paris, an authorized march proceeded peacefully from Republique to Nation, but tensions erupted after police said some 200  "violent individuals" attacked law enforcement after a man was arrested for setting fire to a trash can, according to Paris' police headquarters.

Separately, the country's so-called "Yellow Vest" movement — which gained notoriety eight years ago by violently protesting a proposed fuel tax hike rejected invitations to join the student rally.

Instead, the "Gilets Jaunes" held a banned event at Paris' Place de la Concorde, close to parliament and the presidential Elysee Palace. Two of its leading figures, Benjamin Ball and Solene Azelma Sigaux, were arrested and taken into custody.

Ahead of Saturday's protests, French Interior Minister Laurent Nunez said 70,000 police and security forces would be deployed across the country over the weekend, adding that authorities will "be ready" for "rioters and looters."

The large police presence comes on the heels of weeks of mass demonstrations that have at times grown extremely violent, most notably on October 17, when France's worst rioting in years resulted in more than a billion euros in damage to private and public property.

On Thursday, police made over 600 arrests during a nationwide strike that included blockades and other protest action.

Authorities say that, for the most part, protests have remained peaceful.

But there have continued to be numerous incidents of violent clashes with police, as outgoing President Emmanuel Macron and his government struggle to address public discontent over the rising cost of living.

Although Prime Minister Sebastien Lecornu is scheduled to meet with trade and employee unions on Monday, organizers say the only thing that will quell anger will be for the government to bow to their demands.

"The only thing that will bring the protests to an end is a response to the demands that have been put forward for several weeks by workers, civil servants, young people and pensioners," according to an inter-union coalition statement published on Friday.

This latest round of protests began with students calling for improvements to the national education system and unions joining in to call for wage hikes and tax reforms.

Macron's government has said it intends to raise the minimum wage as well as increase funding for schools and teaching staff. But it finds itself in a tight fiscal spot as debt — currently 119% of GDP — continues to run out of control in the EU's second-largest economy.

#141. How the story of ‘the girl of 100 portraits’ inspired two books

Luc Corlouër made ‘deal’ with Prix Goncourt winner Philippe Jaenada over his research into model Louise Cansot, murdered in Paris in 1949

For almost eight decades the artists’ model Louise Cansot, the subject of the novel winning France’s most prestigious literary prize this year, was one of Paris’s forgotten murder victims.

After her body was found near the Seine in September 1949, city police wrote off the young woman, immortalised as “the girl of 100 portraits”, as loose, libertine and – worse – an unmarried mother. They closed their investigation after just four months having made little effort to find her killer.

But long before the author Philippe Jaenada began researching his, Prix Goncourt-winning novel, L’Inconnue du Quai de Javel (The Unknown Woman of the Quai de Javel), another writer from the Breton village where Cansot was born had already spent decades researching her story.

Luc Corlouër might have felt aggrieved but in a rare show of literary solidarity, the two writers decided they would both publish books. As part of what he calls “the deal”, Corlouër handed Jaenada his notes.

“We agreed we would write our respective books, which are very different. And after all, the star was him, not me,” Corlouër told the Guardian. “By giving him the subject I knew he would make a really superb book. He is a great novelist.”

Cansot, nicknamed Lise, was born in Tréguier, on the Côtes-d’Armor in north-west France, the daughter of a shoe repairer. In 1937, aged 16, she left her family and moved to Paris where her beauty attracted the attention of the celebrated Montparnasse artists, who jostled to have her pose for them.

When war broke out and most of the artists fled south, Cansot remained in Paris working as a film extra, waiter and dancer at the Folies Bergère. She had a five-year-old daughter, Nathalie, whom she left with a nanny in the suburbs until she could raise the money for an apartment, but with the painters gone and no steady income, her situation became increasingly desperate.

On 7 September 1949, her body was found on the Quai du Javel, now known as the Quai André-Citroën, in the 15th arrondissement, south-west of the Eiffel Tower. Detectives initially identified four possible murderers but rapidly filed the case as unsolved.

Jaenada, the author of 17 novels, discovered the story while looking through old newspapers in the French National Library.

By then, Corlouër had already amassed maps, images, photos, paintings, documents, letters and newspaper cuttings on Cansot. His book, La Tragédie du plus beau modèle de Paris (The Tragedy of the Most Beautiful Paris Model) was finally published on 19 August – the same date as Jaenada’s novel – and is based on the documentary information about the case and the victim.

Corlouër says his interest in Cansot was piqued in 1981, when he met an elderly woman at the war memorial in Tréguier. “She told me one name was of a local girl, Annick Cansot, run over by a German truck during the war. When I said: ‘How terrible,’, she said even worse was that her older sister had been murdered in Paris.

“I decided I had to know about Louise Cansot and what happened to her, so I began researching, but my book was a failure. It was a novel about a murder investigation that did not identify the murderer. It was rejected by one publisher after another.

“I thought there was only one person capable of writing such a book: Philippe Jaenada, whom I had already met at a literary salon. Then one day he contacted me. He told me he was interested in the case but said he wouldn’t do anything without me.

“So, I entrusted him with my research. I gave it to him and he did the best investigation imaginable. He dissected all the information and found things I didn’t even think of.”

The original 300-page police report on Cansot’s murder concluded that she was a woman of “ill repute” and newspaper reports speculated that she was a sex worker, alcoholic or drug addict.

In his 530-page book, Jaenada gives a more sympathetic portrayal of the victim, and suggests the name of the man he believes strangled Cansot after inviting her to visit a flat she hoped to rent. His research included reading 74 out of the 75 novels by Georges Simenon featuring Inspector Maigret in 1940s Paris.

Corlouër said it was unsurprising the police had not made the same effort to find the killer back then.

“Lise was a very modern girl at least 50 years ahead of her time,” he said. “She dressed well, was intelligent and free in her choice of lovers. But when she was killed, what journalists and the police saw was a young woman who was a bit libertine, had a baby out of wedlock, had jobs they didn’t think were proper jobs and posed for painters. To them, she had brought what happened on herself.”

Corlouër said he and Jaenada had wanted to rehabilitate Cansot’s reputation after 77 years of slander. “In the police report it’s more or less written that she deserved it. And the defamation continued after her murder,” he said.

Corlouër also revealed how the authors traced Cansot’s daughter Nathalie, now 80, who was raised by another sister, Valentine.

“I don’t want to say too much because it would spoil the end of Philippe’s novel, but when we found her, the first thing she said was she didn’t want to hear a word about her mother and that Lise was a prostitute,” Corlouër said.

“She was raised by her aunt, who was very jealous of Lise and who for years told the girl her mother was disreputable. Nathalie was very surprised when Philippe and I told her this was not true, that her mother was an amazing woman.

“When she heard this, she did not cry. She’s tough like her mother, but she was amazed. So there is a kind of happy ending to this tragic story.”

#137. Polish prosecutors open terrorism investigation into Kraków shooting

Officials say suspect holds far-right views and three people found dead at his home were his parents and sister

Polish prosecutors have opened a terrorism investigation after a 24-year-old man opened fire in a busy market square in Kraków, killing six people including a Briton and injuring 17, in an attack that authorities said was probably inspired by far-right ideologies.

Police confirmed on Sunday that three people found dead at the gunman’s home after the attack on Saturday afternoon were his father, 53, mother, 52, and sister, 21.

As two days of national mourning began, the national prosecutor’s office said on social media it had launched an investigation into “the killing of six people … using a firearm, and the attempted murder of many others, including police officers, this act constituting a terrorist offence”.

The rare mass shooting has shocked a country that was considered safer than many of its western European neighbours, and has deepened concern after several school stabbings in October that left one woman dead and which officials said were linked to extremist content online.

The Kraków suspect, a Polish national, drove into the city’s main square in the heart of the old town, which is usually packed with tourists, at top speed at about 3pm on Saturday. Wearing military-style fatigues and a bulletproof vest, he started shooting at random as he took what the Kraków police spokesperson Katarzyna Cisło described as a “wild ride” around the square.

The interior minister, Marcin Kierwiński, said the suspect, who was apprehended and taken to hospital after being wounded in an exchange of fire with police, was fascinated with far-right ideologies and held “very radical” and “ultra-nationalistic” views.

Four women and two men were killed, including a British citizen, Polish police said. Three of the victims were Polish women and the remaining two have not yet been identified. Five of the wounded are foreign nationals from Britain, Austria, Germany, Portugal and Russia.

Two police ⁠officers were also among the injured, one of whom is in a serious condition. The suspect underwent surgery in hospital, where he is under heavy police guard.

The bodies of the suspect’s parents and sister were found at the family home in Ostrowiec Świętokrzyski, a town about 100 miles north-east of Kraków. Police said they had probably died of gunshot wounds.

Mourners have left candles in tribute to victims in the square, which was still partially cordoned off on Sunday. Flags across the country have been lowered to half mast, as have European flags outside the European Commission in Brussels “in solidarity with the Polish people”, the commission said.

The Polish defense minister, Władysław Kosiniak-Kamysz, called the shooting an “act of terror” on the people of Kraków, Poland’s second largest city, which receives 15 million visitors a year, most of them passing through the main square.

Kraków authorities said the square and adjoining streets “as well as sections along the perpetrator’s escape route will be closed for several hours”. Police in Warsaw, the Polish capital, said security had been bolstered there.

Maria, a 20-year-old employee at a cafe in Warsaw, told Reuters she “almost burst into tears” when she heard about the attack in Kraków. “Recent events – not just this one, but things happening lately – are really affecting our sense of security,” ⁠she said.

The daily newspaper Rzeczpospolita said in an editorial that the mass shooting had brought a “brutal end ​to an era in which Poland was ​considered an island of safety”.

The UK prime minister, Andy Burnham, said: “I’m shocked and saddened by the appalling shooting in Kraków today,. My thoughts are with the families of those who have lost their lives, everyone injured and all those affected by this senseless violence.”

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said his country stood in solidarity with its neighbour, calling the shooting a shocking crime.

France’s Emmanuel Macron described “shock and disbelief following the tragic shooting in Kraków”, while the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, said: “The events in Kraków have deeply affected me,” adding that Germany stood with Poland “as a friend and partner in these difficult times”.

Italy’s prime minister, Giorgia Meloni, expressed condolences to Poland “following the vile and unacceptable act of violence”.

#131. Victoria Barracks in Sydney and Melbourne to remain in public hands after partial backtrack on defence property selloff

Four heritage-listed defence properties to be preserved for community use after backlash from veterans and former PMs

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Richard Marles has denied claims Australia’s defence will be undermined by its decision to repurpose a historical military base near Sydney’s CBD for public use.

The Albanese government announced on Sunday four heritage-listed defence sites, including Sydney’s Victoria Barracks, will remain in public hands after the plan generated considerable public backlash against their sale.

Critics, including veterans and high-profile politicians such as former prime ministers John Howard and Paul Keating, fiercely opposed the decision, with some political opponents questioning if the country’s largest city would be more vulnerable to foreign military and terrorist attacks.

The defence minister said the buildings were mostly used by office workers and no one was “seriously” suggesting the convict-labour built Victoria Barracks was fundamental to Australia’s defence.

“People making the argument know that. I don’t think there’s anyone who’s really seriously suggesting this facility is fundamental to the defence of Australia.”

The defence-owned heritage sites, along with 63 others around the country, were identified in an independent audit as being underutilised by defence, not fit for purpose and costly to maintain, and were earmarked for sale to recoup $3bn in the federal budget.

Ahead of an important cabinet meeting on the budget on Monday, the Albanese government confirmed that four sites – Victoria Barracks in Sydney and Melbourne, and Fort Queenscliff and RAAF Base Point Cook in Victoria – will remain publicly owned and their historic elements preserved for community use after they are vacated by defence personnel.

The prime minister, Anthony Albanese, said parts of the soon-to-be publicly owned sites could still be used for commercial purposes, in line with heritage laws, to help cover their maintenance costs.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

The government will work with the Australian War Memorial once the sites are free to transform the sites into public spaces that tell the history of Australia’s military.

Sydney’s Victoria Barracks was built between 1840 and 1848 and was originally occupied by the British army. It is also the site of the city’s oldest wine cellar.

The shadow defence minister, James Paterson, said questions remain about whether the military should have more of a presence and capability in Melbourne and Sydney.

“While this is a win for Australia’s defence heritage, it is only the beginning. The Coalition will continue to fight to save other critical defence sites, including HMAS Penguin, Lancer Barracks, Irwin Barracks, and more,” he said.

The NSW opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, said the site was an “irreplaceable part of our military heritage that deserves to be protected for future generations”.

“Our military heritage and defence capability must come before a short-term cash grab,” she said.

The Returned & Services League of Australia president, Peter Tinley, said the announcement was a “partial victory”.

“Retaining Victoria Barracks Sydney has never just been about its history. It’s home to the headquarters of forces command, and reservists and cadets train there. That’s a story that should be kept alive.

Two other sites will be partially divested, the government announced. HMAS Penguin in Sydney’s northern beaches will be partially retained by defence with the Angophora Forest transferred to the Sydney Harbour Federation Trust.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

In the Northern Territory, the Stokes Hill fuel installation will be handed back to the Larrakia people, the traditional owners of the lands. The southern portion of the site is a registered sacred site and is protected under law.

The multi-year audit, completed in 2023, was tasked with identifying surplus and costly property holdings, often costing millions to maintain.

The audit found 67 underutilised sites are “draining resources from higher priority needs” for defence.

The sales of the Sydney and Melbourne Victoria Barracks were expected to raise $1.3bn, given their prime locations in the biggest capital cities.

Up to $2.4bn was expected to be raised from the sale of 26 major metropolitan sites, saving about $3bn in upkeep and security costs over 10 years. These include sites in Randwick, Sydney; Sandringham and St Kilda in Melbourne; Petrie Terrace in Brisbane; and Fremantle in Western Australia.

The government hopes the sales will free up land for new housing developments with a long-term target of 6,000 new dwellings.

Defence expects personnel will have vacated six sites by the end of the year and all sites to be divested from within two years.

The announcement comes as cabinet’s expenditure review committee, which determines budget decisions, meets on Monday after the official interest rate was lifted to an 18-year high of 4.85% in a bid to combat persistent inflation this week.

The treasurer, Jim Chalmers, said this week higher fuel prices due to the Middle East conflict were the “primary driver” of inflation rather than the government’s budget.

#130. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#128. Israeli president tasks Gadi Eisenkot with forming government

President Isaac Herzog has picked former Israeli military chief Gadi Eisenkot, leader of an anti-Netanyahu alliance, to take the first shot at trying to form a government. Tricky coalition talks are expected.

Israeli President Isaac Herzog has chosen Gadi Eisenkot to have the first opportunity to form a new Israeli government, according to a statement released by his office on Saturday evening.

The statement said Eisenkot was informed of Herzog's "intention to task him with forming a government" and would be invited to the president's residence, where a formal ceremony will take place on Sunday.

The decision comes after a neck-and-neck race with Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in Israel's October election.

The Eisenkot-led bloc of Zionist parties, fashioned in opposition to Netanyahu, won 48 of the Israeli parliament's 120 seats in October's election, with Netanyahu's camp securing 53.

Israel's Arab parties secured a historic result with 19 parliament seats, with both camps initially promising not to rely on them to form a government ahead of the vote.

Former Israeli military chief Eisenkot is the leader of the centrist Yashar party and heads an anti-Netanyahu alliance.

While Netanyahu ruled out working with Arab parties to gain a majority, both Arab majority lists had announced during the week that they would recommend Eisenkot as the next prime minister.

Political reliance on Arab parties is considered contentious in Israeli politics. However, governments have previously relied on Arab votes to secure a majority.

The Israeli government led by Yitzhak Rabin relied on Arab parties' votes from the opposition until Rabin's assassination in 1995, while the Arab party Ra'am was part of the coalition led by Naftali Bennet and Yair Lapid between 2021 and 2022.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The move is the first step toward what will almost certainly be complicated coalition talks.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a government, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel's longest-serving premier, whose religious-nationalist coalition lost its majority in the election despite emerging as the largest bloc.

Although Eisenkot was runner-up, he managed to secure endorsements from leaders of Arab parties, which are opposed to Netanyahu.

Eisenkot faces a tricky path to forming a government, involving difficult constellations of Arab and ultra-Orthodox parties. He will have 28 days to secure the 61 votes needed to form a government.

Herzog can give Eisenkot a 14-day extension if he comes up short, before the mandate must be offered to another candidate.

If no candidate succeeds, Israel will likely head to a fresh election.

#125. Cold blast brings springtime snow to alpine regions as temperatures plummet across Australia’s south-east

Thredbo, Perisher and Mount Hotham all recorded falls, along with Kunanyi/Mount Wellington in Tasmania

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Snow has fallen across parts of NSW, Victoria and Tasmania as an unseasonal cold blast whips through Australia’s south-east.

Thredbo, Perisher and Mount Hotham all recorded falls, while snow also fell on Kunanyi/Mount Wellington in Hobart.

The snow had been forecast by the Bureau of Meteorology (BoM), and though uncommon, it is not entirely unusual – many of the places hit by snow in the past 24 hours also recorded a fall in November 2022.

The falls brought a rush of posts on social media from those puzzled by the unseasonal spring weather.

“You will not believe this, it’s springtime in Australia, in November, and it’s snowing,” Madeleine McVie, a retail manager at Thredbo, posted on Instagram on Saturday afternoon.

“Like, not just a little bit. It’s fully snowing.”

McVie posted another clip on Sunday morning of her riding empty chairlifts through the park as the snow continued to fall.

Kingston Crows cricket club in Tasmania posted an image of the Kingston Beach Oval on Saturday that showed it coated in hailstones.

The BoM said a weather system, filled with cold air moving north from towards Antarctica, had caused the wintery chill with apparent temperatures much cooler than forecast maximums. South-westerly winds were also causing the apparent or “feels like” temperature to be lower.

At midday, Adelaide, Melbourne, Hobart, Canberra and Sydney all had temperatures that felt cooler than 13C.

The highest temperature at that time was in Adelaide, with a top of 17.5C that felt like 12.3C, with Canberra the lowest (a maximum of 12.9C that felt like 5.3C).

Brisbane, Perth and Darwin, meanwhile, were all enjoying temperatures in the 20s or 30s.

Warmer temperatures are forecast for Monday, and all capital cities are expected to reach at least 20C by Tuesday.

It may be one of the last cold snaps of the year – the BoM, as well as the UN and other bodies, have warned of the likely effects of a “jaw dropping” supercharged El Niño.

The climate crisis, caused by pollution from fossil fuels, is already supercharging extreme weather and killing people. The El Niño is expected to ratchet up the effects even further, and has already begun to affect regions across the planet as weather patterns are altered.

The southern hemisphere summer is predicted to be hotter and drier as a result of the huge El Niño, meaning greater wildfire risk and droughts in Australia and southern Africa.

#121. Psychiatrists and paediatricians among specialists most likely to charge ‘extreme’ fees, thinktank finds

Grattan Institute finds some doctors charge up to triple the fees of their peers – well beyond what can be justified by business running costs

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

More than 2.2 million Australians paid an “extreme” fee to see a specialist last year, with psychiatrists and paediatricians among the most likely to routinely unreasonably charge their patients, according to new research.

Specialists cannot justify exorbitant fees by blaming inadequate government rebates, or the costs of running a business, a report from policy thinktank the Grattan Institute found, as these factors explain only a fraction of the fee increases Australians have faced.

While Medicare rebates fell in real terms by $9 per between 2012 and 2025, specialists increased patient fees by an average of $37 over the same period, recouping the lost subsidy more than four times over. The average specialist fee rose 4% on top of inflation in 2025.

The report, Out of control – how to rein in runaway specialist fees, published on Sunday, defines an extreme fee as being at least triple the official Medicare schedule fee, which is the benchmark price set by the government.

Extreme fees are becoming more common, the report found, with 250,000 extra patients paying them each year, affecting 2.2 million patients in 2025.

“Some specialists routinely charge patients much more than their peers,” the report found.

“The most expensive 5% of psychiatrists charge a median out-of-pocket fee of $669 for an initial consultation. For high-cost paediatricians, it is $362. For high-cost cardiologists, it is $265.11.”

These specialists charge 104%, 212% and 203% more than their peers, respectively.

Among several recommendations, the report calls on the government to force any specialist whose annual fees average more than three times the Medicare schedule fee to repay the government for all rebates claimed that year. Patients would keep their rebate.

This would affect roughly 6% of specialists nationwide (about 2,000 doctors), but more than 60% of private dermatologists and a quarter of obstetrician-gynaecologists.

The median fee for an initial consultation has also risen faster than inflation, with an initial dermatology consultation increasing by $28 to $217.8 between 2023 and 2025, the report found. For psychiatry, it rose by $90, reaching $351.

In 2025, fewer than a quarter of initial consultations were bulk-billed, and fewer than 10% of initial consultations in urology, dermatology, otolaryngology (ear, nose and throat), and psychiatry were bulk-billed.

The lead author of the report, health economist Peter Breadon, said increased price transparency was important but would not be enough to fix what he described as a broken market failing to self-regulate.

In September, legislation passed allowing the government to publish the fees of all specialists on the Medical Costs Finder website, which will commence in 2027. The website has been voluntary for specialists to sign up to, with only about 100 out of 6,300 eligible specialists publicly disclosing their fees since it was established in 2019.

“Some doctors, and some of their advocacy groups, have said self-regulation is the way forward, by appealing to people’s interest in making sure their patients can get the care they need,” Breadon said.

“That kind of approach isn’t working. This is a long-term trend and the real reason is it’s a market where none of the forces that would normally constrain fee growth are working. There’s very little competition because there are not enough specialists to go around, and there is a long public wait.

“It’s also very hard for patients to understand whether a fee is too high, because there is little information about how to evaluate the quality of care. It means regulation is the sensible step.”

The report calls on federal and state governments to invest $500m each year to expand public hospital outpatient clinics; to roll out advice lines for GPs to quickly seek specialist advice so their patients can avoid a referral, wait time and fees; and to impose binding training quotas on medical colleges.

Without reform, Breadon said: “This is not a system that’s going to self-correct.

“It’s been left to run on autopilot for decades.”

The CEO of the Consumers Health Forum, Dr Elizabeth Deveny, said Australians were increasingly “rationing their own care or skipping follow ups simply because the gap costs more than a week of groceries”.

“When the only alternative is a two year public waitlist, people pay because they have run out of options,” she said.

In response to the recommendations, the federal health minister, Mark Butler, told Guardian Australia “all options are on the table to rein in climbing specialist fees”.

The government has established a specialist affordability working group to advise on reforms, of which Breadon is a member.

The Royal College of Physicians president-elect, Dr Julian Kelly, said the peak body would support the government in introducing any regulation to address extreme charging. “The RACP does not defend excessive fees that create barriers to care or place unreasonable financial pressure on patients,” Kelly said.

“Extreme fee charing is unacceptable. We acknowledge the Grattan Institute’s contribution to this important discussion and agree that improving affordability and access to specialist care must remain a national priority.”

#118. Cop31: what is at stake at the UN climate summit and what can it achieve?

Delegates from more than 190 countries are gathering in Turkey for climate talks, but the US will be absent

As the planet faces unprecedented human-caused global heating, heads of government are meeting for the next fortnight for the latest round of climate talks. We ask why it matters and what is likely to be achieved.

From 9-20 November, world leaders, ministers and high-ranking officials from more than 190 countries are expected to convene in Antalya in Turkey, for the annual UN climate talks. This year, the summit will be hosted by Turkey, but in an unusual twist the negotiations will be presided over by Australia in a compromise deal struck after the two countries vied for the presidency.

While nearly all of the 196 original signatories of the 2015 Paris climate agreement are expected to be present, one glaring absence will be the US, whose president, Donald Trump, has withdrawn from Paris.

Australia will take charge of the formal negotiations where countries thrash out the details of work programmes such as the “just transition”, climate finance and putting the world on track to stick as closely as possible to the Paris agreement goal of limiting temperature rises to 1.5C above pre-industrial levels.

Turkey will take charge of the “action agenda”, the voluntary agreements that work towards the Paris goals but are not legally binding: programmes on cutting waste and achieving a circular economy, cutting methane, improving food security, and – perhaps most important – the electrification of the global economy.

The Paris goal of 1.5C has been breached, but the UN argues that rapid action to cut greenhouse gas emissions would limit the “overshoot” and allow for a return to lower temperatures by the end of this century. For that to happen, countries need to break dependence on fossil fuels and adopt low-carbon technologies faster. The two roadmaps from Brazil – on the “transition away from fossil fuels” and on halting deforestation – will present ways of doing so.

Developing countries are also in dire need of help to cope with the hastening impacts of the climate crisis. After the disaster in Nepal, the country has been granted access to the global fund for “loss and damage” – the rescue and rehabilitation of communities stricken by climate-related disasters. But it is not enough, and poor countries also lack the means to adapt their infrastructure to worsening extreme weather.

Rich countries have been cutting their overseas aid, while rising interest rates have loaded further debt on to the poor.

It was not until 2023 at Cop28 that the final agreement directly addressed fossil fuels for the first time, due to long-term strident opposition from producing countries. The agreement included a resolution to “transition away from fossil fuels” but no deadline or plan for doing so. It is still tricky to get fossil fuels directly on to the Cop agenda, which requires consensus from nearly all parties, but this year the issue will be addressed through a non-binding “roadmap” for the global transition away from fossil fuels.

Given the tense and fragile geopolitical situation – some commentators believe we are already in the midst of an unacknowledged third world war, while inflation and a rising far right are upsetting economies – countries might be tempted to settle for a quiet meeting.

But there are hopes for a better outcome, which could include: acceptance of the plans Brazil has produced and a promise to carry them forward; progress on climate finance for developing countries; a resolution to the row over climate science that has plagued recent meetings of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change; and the fleshing out of the “just transition mechanism” designed to help workers reliant on fossil fuels to adjust to a low-carbon economy.

Progress is also possible through Cop-related means outside the formal negotiations. Earlier this year, more than 60 countries, including the UK, the EU and dozens of developing nations, gathered in Colombia to vow to “transition away from fossil fuels”. This “coalition of the willing” could be boosted at Cop31 by new members, as well as fresh impetus from the recent success of political movements in several countries – Brazil, Canada, Serbia, Hungary and the US in states that decisively rejected Donald Trump at the midterms.

Several prominent members of the US Congress may attend, along with businesspeople and US civil society groups, so there will be representation beyond the White House’s absence. Trump appears intent on boosting fossil fuel interests as much as possible in the US, but with oil prices still high after his war in Iran, the economics of low-carbon energy look ever more favourable.

The geopolitics and the technology. Although the winds of conflict have upended international relations, they have also shown countries can benefit from the energy security that results from decentralised renewable generation. China’s oil imports, for instance, are likely to be down about 12% this year owing to a massive uptake of electric vehicles, and its renewable generation capacity has now outstripped coal. This is saving billions of dollars for consumers and businesses as the oil price soars.

Solar is booming in Africa at last, while in Pakistan a consumer-led rooftop revolution now produces about a quarter of the electricity used. Close to half of new cars in countries from Vietnam to some EU members are now electric.

Electrification will be a key focus of Cop31, and the high efficiency of electric technology means it could reduce the amount of primary energy use by half across the world, according to University of Oxford professor Jan Rosenow.

Politically, the re-election of Lula as president in Brazil has also cheered climate supporters. Lula will attend Cop, his first international visit since re-election, and is likely to be a fiery presence, showing progressive parties around the world how to win while championing the climate.

#116. Who’s who at Cop31: key players at the climate talks

What are the relationships and dominant interests in play, as the summit kicks off after a bitter row over its hosting?

Hosting Cop was a key pledge from Australia’s prime minister, Anthony Albanese in 2021, while in opposition, before his successful election campaign. But arcane rules meant the country had to wait its turn to bid and was then embroiled in an angry row with Turkey over the hosting. The final compromise put Australia in charge of the negotiations which will take place in the Turkish city of Antalya. The bitter taste left by the row lingers and Australia has seemed to some a rather half-hearted host. The country’s media has been much quieter on Cop than previous host countries, and although Albanese will speak at the opening of the leaders’ segment, he is unlikely to stay long.

Chris Bowen, Australia’s climate and energy minister, will be in daily charge of the negotiations. As aspects of Australia’s energy supply also fall under his portfolio, he has been in the sometimes uncomfortable position of having to boost fossil fuels while insisting on the need for a clean energy transition.

Yet Australia has a positive story to tell on decarbonisation: while remaining one of the world’s major fossil fuel exporters, the country has also invested heavily in renewables, and solar power has now expanded so much that consumers are sometimes offered free electricity.

Recep Tayyip Erdoğan, Turkey’s autocratic president, showed little interest in climate and environmental concerns before bidding to host this year’s Cop summit.

In the last five years, Cop has also been held in Egypt, the United Arab Emirates, Azerbaijan, all in the same broad region. This has given an outsize role to the Arab group of countries, who are neighbours in the region and have a strong interest in ensuring that fossil fuel interests – including their lobbyists – are well represented.

Australia runs the overall direction of the negotiations, and Turkey’s government, with the environment minister, Murat Kurum, has control of the action agenda, a programme of voluntary initiatives. Erdoğan has used this to push forward a key interest of his wife, Emine Erdoğan, in cutting waste and promoting the circular economy. She has called for an end to “throwaway culture” and for recycling instead of landfill – though Turkey still has sites that leak vast quantities of methane.

Fatih Birol, the head of the International Energy Agency and one of the world’s leading energy economists, is understood to be one of the advisers behind this year’s emphasis on electrification, displacing fossil fuels with electricity for heating, transport and industrial uses. This could halve the amount of energy we need, and is the next biggest step, after boosting renewable energy generation, needed for a low-carbon world.

The recently re-elected President Lula da Silva will come to Cop31 for his first trip abroad after winning his historic fourth term, and will bring two roadmaps, one a global plan for the transition away from fossil fuels, the other for halting and reversing deforestation. These are non-binding, and contain no deadlines or firm requirements from governments, but show a series of steps that governments can take to achieve these goals.

Xi Jinping, the president of China, will not come to this year’s Cop, but his country’s rapid energy and industrial transformation will determine the course of the planet’s future. China is the world’s second biggest economy and the biggest emitter of greenhouse gases, making up nearly a third of global annual carbon output, owing mainly to the use of fossil fuels to power its explosive economic growth for the last 25 years. But it has also invested in renewable energy, which is reshaping the global energy business.

Xi may not be present in Antalya, but the top officials he sends will know that petrostates – including Russia, Saudi Arabia and the UAE – are his important allies. However, countries pushing for a low-carbon future may be more important still – as China’s customers.

For several months this year, India’s prime minister, Narendra Modi, mulled an intriguing proposition: to host the Cop33 UN climate summit in 2028. In the end, India withdrew its bid in April without specifying why, leaving the field open. India is torn between its burgeoning clean energy sector – it now has the world’s third biggest installed capacity of renewable energy, after China and the US – and its coal sector, which still supplies about 70% of electricity, and has close links to Modi.

Bhupender Yadav, India’s environment minister, who leads its Cop delegations, enjoys a mutually supportive relationship with the UK’s Ed Miliband, and a reputation as a forward thinker.

The attention of Donald Trump has been fixed on the crucial midterm elections, a verdict on his second term as president that has shown Republicans the electoral dangers of an unpredictable White House. No US delegation is expected at Cop31, and soon Trump’s withdrawal of the country from the UN framework convention on climate change will mean none can take part.

Trump’s shadow was felt at Cop30 in Belem last year, as he started to dismantle the US’s renewable energy sector and boost fossil fuels, setting the US on course to start increasing greenhouse gas emissions again, after decades in which they fell. But his administration’s unpopularity with the US electorate, and a drought and fire-filled summer, has bolstered the pro-climate stance of scores of Democratic congresspeople and state governors, despite a campaign of “climate hushing” before the midterms. Some of them, and a scattering of business leaders, may attend Cop31.

Andy Burnham will attend Cop for the first time this year, after a mixed record on the climate since taking office. During the record-breaking heatwaves and droughts of this summer, the British prime minister was criticised for failing to speak out on the climate crisis. He subsequently made strong interventions affirming his support for the UK’s target of reaching net zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050, but then backed the Jackdaw gasfield. No decision has been announced on the Rosebank oilfield yet.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

From December, Burnham will have a much bigger opportunity to craft climate policy on the international stage: the UK will take over the revolving presidency of the influential G20 group, which comprises the world’s biggest developed and developing economies. The Guardian understands that the UK will emphasise building resilience against the impacts of the climate crisis as a key focus of the G20 for its year-long presidency.

Miliband, now foreign secretary, and a veteran of many Cops in government and opposition, is expected to accompany the prime minister to the leaders’ summit segment of Cop31. Miliband was hailed, in public and private, by many participants last year for his patient bridge-building that helped Brazil to rescue a deal from the fractious final hours of Cop30.

At this year’s UN general assembly, he also briefed other governments on the findings of the UK’s joint intelligence committee, which warned that the impact of collapsing ecosystems around the world posed a national security risk, threatening food shortages, mass migration and potential nuclear war.

The energy and net zero secretary, Miatta Fahnbulleh, will head the UK’s Cop31 delegation for the final negotiations in the last days of this fortnight of talks, after the leaders have departed.

The EU has faced a quandary over its climate convictions in recent years. Far-right populists are on the rise, pushing climate denialism, and the bloc has backed down from some key regulations, watering down its green deal package, loosening the strings on corporate climate liabilities, delaying rules on trade in goods from deforested areas, and extending the phase-in of new car rules. The failure of Europe’s car industry to move decisively to electric vehicles – preferring lobbying against emissions standards, which has turned out to be a poor strategy as consumers turn in droves to cheap-to-run EVs – has led to threats to slap tariffs on China.

The EU commission president, Ursula von der Leyen called this summer’s record-breaking heatwaves and drought a “summer of truth” that must be answered: she is now confirmed for the summit. Wopke Hoekstra, the Dutch climate commissioner, will oversee the final stages of the talks for the EU, and is expected to make competition with China a key focus.

Cop31 will mark the final outing at a UN climate summit for António Guterres, the UN secretary general, who has regularly intervened powerfully on this subject. A successor to Guterres has still not yet been chosen – the US has tried to get Viktor Orbán, the far-right former president of Hungary, into the role, or a former minister from the United Arab Emirates, Lana Nusseibeh.

Simon Stiell, the determined executive secretary of the UNFCCC, will oversee the talks, with an added twist to his job this year, having to liaise between the two co-hosts as well as the fractious parties.

Saudi failed to confirm with the Guardian before Cop who would lead its delegation, but the country’s recent behaviour at international climate meetings tells its own story. The country has a long history of obstructing the UN climate negotiations, though its behaviour was sometimes muted when the US intervened. With Trump in the White House, Saudi has felt emboldened to interfere further, with allies including Russia, Venezuela, members of the Arab group, and the Like-Minded Developing Countries grouping.

At Cop30, the Saudi delegation refused to allow even an oblique mention of the pledge to “transition away from fossil fuels”, made at Cop28 in Dubai in 2023, in the final text until the final moments.

At recent meetings of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, Saudi led a small group of countries – including China, India, Russia and Kenya – that fought to delay publication of the next comprehensive assessment of climate science. Publication of the landmark report, which is expected to warn of catastrophic impacts from further temperature rises, was expected in 2028, in time for Cop33 at which countries will assess their progress on meeting emissions-cutting goals under the Paris agreement, a process known as the “global stocktake”. Delaying it by a year or more, as Saudi wants, would mean a loss of urgency for the global stocktake, and could help countries to get away with inadequate measures on decarbonisation.
