#114. Ebola: Why is there still no vaccine?

Ebola has been known about for decades, but there is still no vaccine against the Bundibugyo strain of the virus causing the current outbreak. New research raises hopes of protection against the various types of Ebola.

1976: A hitherto unknown disease breaks out near the Ebola river in the country then known as Zaire, now the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). This was the first scientifically documented Ebola epidemic.

Now, 50 years on, the DRC once again finds itself at the center of a major Ebola outbreak. This time the pathogen is the less common Bundibugyo type of the genus Orthoebolavirus. The virus was identified in 2007 during an outbreak in Bundibugyo, Uganda. Another outbreak occurred in the DRC in 2012.

How can a virus that has been known for decades continue to infect thousands of people, yet there is still no licensed vaccine or official treatment?

Torsten Feldt, a doctor of tropical medicine at Düsseldorf's University Hospital, sees this as a missed opportunity. "We had come across the Bundibugyo virus in two previous outbreaks: 2007 in Uganda and 2012 in DR Congo. So we had enough time to develop vaccines and therapeutic options. But we didn't make use of this opportunity, meaning crucial time was lost during the current outbreak."

Leonie Mayer from the German Center for Infection Research (DZIF) and the Institute for Infection Research and Vaccine Development at the University Medical Center in Hamburg-Eppendorf agrees. "They could of course have developed a vaccine and run pre-clinical trials before," Mayer told DW. She stressed, however, that their effectiveness can only be assessed and confirmed during an actual outbreak.

Mayer is the lead author of a recently published study that examined whether Ervebo, an Ebola vaccine that has already been licensed, can be used against other types of Ebola as well.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

From an immunological point of view, and compared to — for example — influenza or HIV, Ebola is a very worthwhile candidate for a vaccine. Influenza viruses are constantly mutating, and the human immunodeficiency virus is particularly good at evading our immune defenses. Ebola is far more predictable.

"I think — and perhaps it's kind of a blessing in disguise — that it will be relatively straightforward to develop a vaccine against Bundibugyo," says Mayer.

Herein lies the paradox. From a scientific point of view, Ebola is a good vaccine candidate, yet there is still no licensed form of prevention against the Bundibugyo variant that is currently circulating.

Mayer explains that vaccines cannot be developed as a precautionary measure against every potential pathogen. Lack of funding or the prioritization of other pathogens often have a role to play. Many researchers had assumed that a fresh outbreak of the Sudan Ebola virus was more likely, and vaccine development for this strain is more advanced.

Another part of the problem is that Ebola is not one single virus. The known, highly dangerous orthoebolaviruses include Ebola virus (EBOV), previously called the Zaire Ebola virus (ZEBOV), the Sudan virus (SUDV), and the Bundibugyo virus (BDBV). The existing vaccine, Ervebo, was developed and licensed for use against the Zaire variant. But there is still no approved protection against the others.

This is why the existing Ebola vaccine has been of only limited use to people in the areas of the current outbreak.

Another factor may also be relevant: The Bundibugyo virus appears to be less aggressive than the Zaire variant. At first, that sounds like good news, but it may make it harder to contain, as people remain mobile for longer after being infected, and can pass on the virus without realizing.

In the recently published study, Mayer's research team from the DZIF investigated whether the already licensed Ebola vaccine could trigger an immune response not only against EBOV, but against other types of Ebola as well. They found cross-reactive T-cell responses against Bundibugyo, and also, to a lesser extent, against the Sudan virus.

For Mayer, this is already an important finding: "We now know that the immune cells can most likely also recognize the Bundibugyo virus." But she also observes: "Our data does not enable us to answer the question of whether this immune response actually provides protection, or if it results in a milder progression of the disease." This is currently an important gap in our knowledge.

If there's a vaccine that has already been licensed, and indications that it could protect people, why isn't it simply being deployed?

Mayer explains the dilemma. For one thing, she says, it's not ethical to deploy a vaccine if you're not sure whether or how well it works. Also, if it were used on a large scale, it would no longer be possible to establish whether it actually works: A reduction in cases could also be a result of other measures, or the epidemic might already have passed its peak.

Jürgen May, a specialist in tropical diseases at the Bernhard Nocht Institute, also warns against mass vaccination: "Mass vaccination without proof of efficacy could create a false sense of security, forfeit trust, and, if it does not provide sufficient protection, make study impossible."

It's very important for there to be acceptance on the ground. "If we inoculate people with a vaccine on a large scale, and afterwards it turns out that it was nothing like as effective as we expected, it's very difficult to communicate that to people on the ground," Mayer says. In a worst-case scenario, people could subsequently refuse inoculation with more effective vaccines.

But the current outbreak also shows that medical innovation alone is not enough. Conflicts, difficult transport routes, attacks on healthcare facilities, and gaps in contact tracing all make it harder to contain.

Torsten Feldt says identifying Bundibugyo is no longer a problem in itself. What is often limiting is the practical availability of diagnostic tools. "In remote or conflict-affected regions, samples often have to be transported over long distances," he explains.

Here we see another Ebola paradox. The world is getting ever better at diagnosing infections, but it still struggles to contain outbreaks fast enough. The authors of an article in the current issue of Nature come to the same conclusion: The problem is often not identifying cases, but the speed with which people react. Finding Ebola is often easier now than stopping Ebola, they write.

Many researchers believe the current crisis could potentially also provide a great opportunity. The so-called Tumaini study, which is testing different vaccine candidates, has now been launched in the DRC.

These include Ervebo, developed by Oxford University, and a Moderna mRNA vaccine. The study aims to provide the first reliable data about their efficacy against the Bundibugyo virus.

Whether these vaccines could still help control the current outbreak is uncertain. There is hope, but experts estimate that, realistically, it will be several months before any such vaccine is available for widespread use; perhaps not until mid-2027.

"Ideally, we will develop cross-reactive pan-Ebola vaccines," Christof Geldmacher, a co-author of the DZIF study, says of their long-term plans. This is also something that is being worked on. For now, though, the focus is on developing an effective vaccine against the Bundibugyo variant of the virus.

This article has been translated from German.

#113. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#110. Duchess of Sussex’s estranged half-brother ‘person of interest’ in child sexual abuse inquiry

Philippine official says there are ‘signs that point to Thomas Markle Jr’s involvement in crime’, but he’s not yet being treated as a suspect

The Duchess of Sussex’s estranged half-brother, Thomas Markle Jr, has been named as a “person of interest” in the Philippine authorities’ inquiry into child sexual abuse image allegations made against her father, Thomas Markle Sr.

The country’s interior and local government secretary, Jonvic Remulla, told a press conference in Manila there were “signs that point that the son is involved in the crime as well”.

He added that Markle Jr, 60, who alerted local police and the FBI to images he found in his father’s belongings, was not yet being treated as a suspect. His alleged involvement is not thought to be directly linked to the material he handed to the police.

More than 7,000 photos and one video – all of under-age males and females of Asian, Latin and European descent, with some as young as 10 and 11 – were uncovered, Remulla said.

He described the images as “the most deplorable displays of impunity towards sexual activity that I have ever seen”.

Markle Sr, 82, who is estranged from Meghan, has reportedly been moved into a care home for dementia patients in the US where he is now living.

Remulla said: “We’re determining if [the] son is involved … There are signs that point that the son is involved in the crime as well but only as a person of interest so far.”

He added: “We will know in the next few days.”

Markle Sr, who returned to the US from the Philippines in May, has admitted paying for sex in the Philippines but he has strongly denied possessing child abuse imagery, and suggested he had been “set up”. His son remains in Cebu City in the Philippines.

Remulla has previously described Markle Sr as “a possible sex offender of the grossest kind”.

Markle Jr reported his father after claiming to have found four storage boxes at his father’s home in the Philippines containing “hundreds, if not thousands” of child sex abuse images.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

Police and FBI agents have since been investigating whether Markle Sr was in possession of any illegal images while living in the Philippines and in Mexico, where he previously lived, and where other illegal images were reportedly found in one of his storage units.

Markle Jr was recently interviewed at his home in Cordova, Cebu, as part of an international, multi-agency meeting via Microsoft Teams, according to the Sun, which first published the claims. He gave his statement to an FBI agent based in Florida, who specialises in child sex abuse cases.

In a statement issued when the allegations against her father were first published, Meghan said she was “deeply distressed” by the claims. She said she had been informed of disturbing allegations against her father in 2018 and had kept him from having contact with her children, Prince Archie, seven, and Princess Lilibet, five, to protect them.

#108. The young Somali woman changing how the world sees Mogadishu

Somalia has the longest coastline in mainland Africa

As the sun rises over the Indian Ocean, waves lap onto the golden sands while palm fronds rustle in the cool sea breeze. Welcome to... Mogadishu.

This is the image that one young Somali woman wants the world to have of her home city - in contrast to the one of suicide attacks and young men wielding AK-47s that many people currently associate with Somalia's capital.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader is among a growing number of local guides taking visitors beyond the headlines to explore Mogadishu's beauty spots, historic sites and culture.

Her favourite spot is Jazeera beach, where visitors can enjoy the sea before heading to nearby sand dunes.

"It's one of the tourist attractions where we bring our guests. It's a nice place," says the 23-year-old.

The dunes, she adds, remind some visitors of the Sahara.

But her tours are about more than scenic views. She also takes visitors to the fish market, where they discover the variety of marine life available along Somalia's extensive coastline and to parts of the city that reflect its history as a busy Indian Ocean trading centre, with the influences of both its Ottoman and Italian former rulers.

Visitors can also sample Somali food, including camel meat, samosas and traditional tea.

Sucdi says many arrive in the city with expectations shaped by years of hearing about the insecurity.

"Mostly, they are surprised by how beautiful the country is and how welcoming people are," she says.

At the fish market, she says, visitors are particularly impressed by the variety of fish species.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader wants visitors to experience the "beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu"

Guiding tours runs in Sucdi's family. Her father worked in the profession for over three decades, taking journalists, photographers and visitors from different countries around Somalia.

He introduced her to the business nearly three years ago.

"I started this job as a hobby, but it has become something that I like and am passionate about," she says.

She now works alongside her father and a team of guides, building on his experience while using social media to reach a wider audience.

She shares videos of her tours on TikTok, offering people outside Somalia a glimpse of places they may never have imagined visiting.

She is also considering producing content in English to reach audiences who do not speak Somali.

A wide variety of fish are available in Mogadishu's markets

Tourism remains a relatively new field in Somalia, she says, with only a handful working as guides in Mogadishu.

The business is seasonal, but Sucdi says this year has been particularly encouraging, although numbers are still fairly low. Having five tourists a week is considered a success but she is confident that numbers will grow.

However, she says many visitors are still concerned about security.

Since the collapse of Somalia's central government in 1991, Mogadishu has endured decades of civil war, destruction and displacement. Much of the city centre was reduced to rubble, and basic services and infrastructure largely broke down.

But following the withdrawal of the Islamist militant group al-Shabab from most of the city in 2011, and the establishment of the federal government in 2012, Mogadishu has undergone a gradual transformation.

Returning members of the Somali diaspora, local entrepreneurs and investors helped fuel a construction boom, with new hotels, restaurants, businesses and residential developments reshaping the capital.

Today, Mogadishu is a growing commercial hub, although insecurity and the legacy of conflict remain part of everyday life.

About 10,000 tourists visited Somalia in 2024, a 50% increase on the previous year, according to the country's tourism department.

Many visitors ask about their safety before travelling and often encounter security checkpoints around the city.

But she says their experience on the ground is generally positive.

Lido beach is one of the most popular places for people to relax in Mogadishu

"What they heard from the news and everywhere else is not the same as what they see in the country," she says.

Although some question whether Mogadishu is safe enough for a young woman to be working as a tour guide, Sucdi is determined to continue.

She wants visitors to experience a Somalia that is rarely seen.

"I would want them to see the beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu, and not the side that they heard from the media, which is explosions and bombs," she says.

Somalia has Africa's longest mainland coastline, stretching more than 3,000km (1,860 miles) along the Indian Ocean and Gulf of Aden.

Its beaches and sand dunes sit alongside historic coastal cities such as Mogadishu, whose culture reflects centuries of Indian Ocean trade.

For some tourists, that contrast between the country's reputation and what they experience can be striking.

American travel content creator Chris Reese, who goes by the name Authentic Travelling, has visited Mogadishu twice.

His first trip, almost a year ago, was a solo visit. He enjoyed it so much that he decided to return with a group of friends, who had seen videos of his visit on social media.

"I had such a positive experience - I really enjoyed seeing the country for the first time - that I opted to bring back a group of my friends," he says.

What stands out for Chris is the city's coastal setting, its food and atmosphere.

"It is such a beautiful tropical place. I love the vibe, the weather and the food," he says.

The sand dunes near Mogadishu remind some visitors of the Sahara Desert

During the group's visit, they enjoy lunch overlooking the Indian Ocean before heading out to explore the beaches and sand dunes.

"The desert is beautiful and picturesque. It is also amazing that it's barely a 30-minute drive from Mogadishu," he says.

Before his first trip, Chris says, much of what he knew about Somalia came from international news coverage.

"If you only read the news reports about Somalia, you would be scared to visit. And honestly, that's what I saw in the media before I came, mostly negative headlines."

But visiting the country challenged those perceptions.

"Coming here, on my very first trip, showed me that there is so much more to this country than what is shown in the headlines," he says.

Romanian Mihaela Patap says she prefers to visit new countries without preconceived ideas

Mihaela Patap, from Romania, loves Somalia's natural beauty.

Although she has spent only a few hours in Mogadishu, she has already visited the sand dunes and taken in the Indian Ocean coastline.

"The desert was so beautiful to see. I have also enjoyed the beach, and the ocean. It is inspiring," she says.

Having travelled to Somalia from neighbouring Ethiopia, she says she has been struck by the people's friendliness.

"I don't see anything dangerous. I feel safe and the people are warm," she says.

An experienced traveller, Mihaela says she prefers to visit new countries without having preconceived ideas.

"I travel the world to see new places, to see countries, to see the differences. I don't take a picture in my mind to compare the reality with. I'm just expecting the new to come to me," she says.

She hopes to share her experience and encourage others to visit Somalia.

"I'm going to post my pictures and say good things about what I'm experiencing. I'm going to promote it," she says.

She is continuing a family profession while building a business in an industry with few women and using digital platforms to reach audiences far beyond Mogadishu.

"Somalia still has a long way to go before tourism becomes a major industry," she says.

"Through my tours, I am giving visitors an opportunity to experience its landscapes, our culture and people beyond the familiar negative headlines."

For her, every visitor is another opportunity to tell a fuller story of Mogadishu.

#107. Poland: Gunman kills 6 in Krakow main square, police say

A suspect who fired from a car killed at least six people, including a British tourist, in Krakow's main market square. Officials said the man was fascinated with "radical" and "far-right" ideologies.

At least six people were killed and 16 injured in a shooting on the main market square in the southern Polish city of Krakow on Saturday, officials said.

The suspect, a 25-year-old Polish man who allegedly acted alone, was wounded during an exchange of fire with police, authorities said.

"Until now in Poland we haven't had such drastic cases of terrorism," Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk told reporters in Krakow.

"That's what we have to call this cruel attack on people's lives here in Krakow," he added.

The gunman aimed at random people as he drove through the city's main square, which is a popular tourist attraction.

"At around 3 p.m. (1400 GMT), a man who had driven into the market square in a car began shooting at random people from inside the vehicle," police said on X, adding officers were among the wounded.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The perpetrator was shot by police ‌officers and is currently being ‌transported ​to a hospital," police said. Authorities later clarified that the man drove to multiple spots in the square and opened fire at each with an automatic weapon.

Later Saturday evening, Polish police chief Marek Boron said a British citizen was among those killed.

"There are six fatalities: four women and two men; three Polish women, one British national, and two people who have not yet been identified," Boron told reporters.

Krakow police had earlier said that five of the 16 people injured in the shooting were non-Polish nationals. An Interior Ministry spokesperson confirmed that they came from Austria, Germany, Portugal, Russia and Britain.

On Saturday night, Tusk said that the bodies of two women and a man were found in the suspected shooter's home by police.

There was "much to suggest that they are members of the [suspect's] immediate family."

The residence is located in Ostrowiec Swietokrzyski, a city about 150 kilometers (93 miles) northeast of Krakow.

"We have the next acts of this tragedy," Tusk told reporters.

Polish Interior Minister Marcin Kierwinski said that the suspect, whom he declined to identify, was "fascinated by far-right ideologies" and held "ultranationalistic" and "very radical" views.

The man wore military-style fatigues and a bulletproof vest and used a long gun in the attack, said Katarzyna Cislo, a spokesperson for the Krakow police,

Numerous other weapons and ammunition were found in the suspected gunman's car.

Prime Minister Tusk posted on X that police were investigating the attack and called it a "tragic" incident.

Tusk later said Poland would observe two days of mourning for the victims of the shooting.

Polish President Karol Nawrocki wrote on X that his thoughts were with the victims and wounded, as well as their families.

The European Union's top diplomat, Kaja Kallas, said the shooting was a tragedy that will be felt far beyond Poland, adding that "Poland does not stand alone."

"I am deeply saddened by the events in Krakow. My thoughts are with the victims and their families," German Chancellor Friedrich Merz wrote on X, adding that "Germany stands alongside Poland as a friend and partner during these difficult times."

Krakow is located about 250 kilometers (155 miles) from Warsaw, the capital of Poland.

With a population of over 800,000, Krakow is Poland's second-largest city, as well as a popular tourist destination.

#106. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#103. With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

In the aftermath of the Republican Party's sweeping defeat in Tuesday's midterm elections, Donald Trump said he would be willing to work with the newly ascendent Democrats in Congress.

On Friday, however, he elevated Stephen Miller, his bare-knuckled political operative and longtime adviser, to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff.

Far from an olive branch to Congress, Miller's promotion could be a sign that the president is girding for political battle by installing a partisan firebrand in the top White House job.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our president and defend this magnificent republic," Miller posted on X, adding that he was "honored beyond words" to be Trump's pick for the job.

One of the most powerful positions in Washington, the chief of staff is gatekeeper to the president, agenda-setter in the White House and chief negotiator with Congress and other outside political interests.

"Stephen Miller's been with me since the beginning," Trump said in comments to reporters after announcing the move on his Truth Social website. "He gets it."

Watch: Trump says he appointed Stephen Miller âto make a changeâ

Miller is best known for being the driving force behind the Trump administration's aggressive immigration enforcement policies, which has included ramping up funding for enforcement operations across the US.

He made headlines when he called Alex Pretti, who was shot and killed by federal immigration officials during a massive deportation operation in Minneapolis, a "domestic terrorist" and an "assassin". He would later walk back those words, saying federal agents "may not have been following" proper protocol.

He has also reportedly played a leading role in the US military operations in Venezuela and against suspected drug trafficking operations in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific, which critics have condemned as extra-judicial killings.

Miller joined Trump's political operation early in his 2016 presidential bid as a strategist and incendiary warm-up speaker at his raucous campaign rallies.

He was one of the few senior White House staffers to persevere through the entirety of his first term. He stayed loyal to Trump during his political exile following attempts to overturn his ballot-box defeat in 2020 failed. He then returned in the influential role of deputy chief of staff for policy in Trump's second term.

Wiles, who co-managed the president's successful 2024 re-election campaign, developed a reputation as a cool professional who avoided the White House staffing upheavals, dramatic policy reversals and personality clashes that typified Trump's first presidential term.

The president, however, began souring on Wiles in recent months, as his political standing in the US declined, culminating in Tuesday's electoral rout. He also reportedly chafed at news stories indicating that those in Wiles' orbit were landing lucrative consulting jobs through their connections to the president.

Trump names Stephen Miller as new White House chief of staff

'Trump's psyche': The aide driving president's most controversial policies

What has Stephen Millerâs role been in Trumpâs second term?

While Trump praised Miller's effectiveness as a manager on Friday, working with Congress could prove to be a challenge.

It is difficult to overstate how disliked Miller is by most Democrats â and some Republicans.

"Stephen Miller is a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands," Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries wrote on X. "His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor."

Earlier this year, Miller posted on social media that James Talarico, who had just secured the Democratic nomination to run for Senate in Texas, was "their first transgender Senate candidate".

The Democratic Party offered a terse, obscenity laced reply, telling Miller to shut up.

Now Miller will have to negotiate with a US Senate that includes a newly elected Talarico as one of its rising stars, after an election in which polls found that 58% of voters believe the administration's immigration enforcement actions had "gone too far" and Hispanics decisively turned against Republican candidates.

On Wednesday, in a social media post attempting to explain the Republican midterm defeats, Vice-President JD Vance - who is an early frontrunner for the Republican 2028 presidential nomination - wrote that he was "open to the idea that we should change enforcement - by focusing more on businesses that hire illegal labour, for instance".

That now seems less likely with an immigration hardline taking the White House helm.

Thom Tillis, the North Carolina Republican senator who is retiring at the end of the year, has been particularly vocal in condemning Miller. He has called him "incompetent" and "the architect of some of the dumbest proposals from the Trump White House".

Other Republicans, however, have sung his praises.

"Stephen Miller has been a true conservative warrior since his days as a Senate staffer, a staunch defender of law and order against illegal invasions and leftist lawlessness, and a steadfast fighter for President Trump for over a decade," Utah Senator Mike Lee posted on X after Trump's announcement.

At a time when congressional Democrats are promising to launch new investigations and oversight into the Trump administration, it appears that a "warrior" and a "fighter" is the kind of person the president wants to have running his White House.

#102. As COP31 Antalya kicks off, can world move beyond big promises?

Droughts, floods and extreme heat are upping the pressure on delegates at the UN climate talks in Antalya, Turkey. Can countries move beyond pledges to concrete action?

With its pulsing music, flashy images and barrage of special effects, the key message in Antalya's marketing video is clear: Turkey, the host of the 31st edition of the UN climate talks, is cool, stylish, modern — and eco-conscious.

Both President Recep Tayyip Erdogan and his wife, Emine, make an appearance, with Turkey's first lady calling climate change "one of the most visible manifestations of the burden human activity has placed on our planet."

From November 9-20, delegates from nearly every country in the world are coming together in the Mediterranean city of Antalya to chart a way out of the climate crisis. Turkey is the official host, and Australia will lead the negotiations. But the issues they'll confront during those 12 days are far more serious than the showy marketing campaign make them out to be.

Severe drought, extreme heat, wildfires and record-low water levels in Europe's main waterways — summer across the continent was dominated by climate emergencies.

And in late August, a series of flash floods triggered by a rock-ice avalanche in Nepal and Tibet washed away entire villages, killing more than 1,400 people and leaving thousands missing. Climate experts have linked the disaster to melting ice and a warming climate.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Globally, carbon emissions are still rising: the UN has concluded that it is no longer possible to keep global temperature rise to 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 Fahrenheit) above pre-industrial levels, the goal agreed at COP21 in 2015.

The global transition away from polluting fossil fuels like coal, oil and gas will be at the center of attention in Antalya — once again. To date, there's been no consensus on whether and how to transition rapidly away from this main source of global warming.

Despite the setbacks, the world has made some progress thanks to the 2015 Paris Agreement.

David Victor, a climate expert at the University of California San Diego, pointed out that before then, the planet was hurtling toward an average temperature rise of between 3-3.5 C.

If current climate commitments are fully implemented, the latest UN projections show a rise of up to 2.6 C. It's a significant step forward, even though warming of that magnitude will still have worldwide consequences.

"There's no question that we could be doing more," said Victor, though he cautioned against pinning too much on this year's summit.

All agreements at COP need to be unanimous, meaning individual states can quash any sweeping resolutions. Added to that challenge are the ongoing wars, trade conflicts and other economic problems swirling around this year's negotiations.

"It's like a giant Christmas tree in which everybody's hung their different ornaments, and the tree is groaning and more ornaments are being put on it," said Victor. "We have to look at it for what it is: It's a place where people talk about stuff."

Instead of waiting for all COP member states to come to an agreement, Victor sees a better chance for change in what he called "clubs" — smaller alliances of governments and companies working together to advance actual solutions, whether it be in aviation, shipping or electromobility.

"The Paris process is important. It has to go forward," he told DW. "But all the work is going to be done in clubs."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

An example of this approach was last April's first ever International Conference on Transitioning Away from Fossil Fuels in Santa Marta, Colombia.

Colombia and the Netherlands invited representatives from 57 countries and key sectors to discuss ways to move the world away from coal, oil and gas, after an agreement on a similar plan failed at the 2025 climate summit due to opposition from several oil-producing states.

With an eye to the ongoing trade conflicts and energy crisis, hosts Turkey and Australia have included green industrialization and the clean energy transition among their aims. One goal is to increase the share of electricity in global energy consumption to 35% by 2035, up from around 20% today.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A recent analysis by the International Energy Agency found accelerating electrification could cut fuel-importing countries' energy bills by more than $400 billion (€357 billion) by 2035, compared to 2025 levels. The idea could appeal to regions dependent on fossil fuel imports — among them Germany and the European Union.

Ahead of COP31, German Environment Minister Carsten Schneider said Germany and the EU wanted to spark new momentum in the "global phaseout of fossil fuels."

It's wouldn't just be good for the climate, but a chance for many countries to boost their economies and reduce dependence on energy imports, added Schneider in a statement.

Oil-producing states like Saudi Arabia, however, aren't interested in deals that will undermine their business model. At recent UN climate conferences, they've repeatedly blocked any final agreements including phaseout plans.

Even China, despite its massive expansion of wind and solar energy over the last 20 years, could be against the idea. The economic superpower traditionally rejects any outside demands, proposals or interference concerning its national decisions on energy policy.

Every country in the world is affected by climate change, with loss and damage calculated in the hundreds of billions from heat, drought and storms. Worst hit are vulnerable countries in the Global South and island nations, who are at risk of disappearing as sea levels rise.

That's where money comes into play, with developing countries calling for more support to protect their citizens from the harms of global warming. At the 2024 UN climate talks in Baku, Azerbaijan, industrialized nations agreed to free up at least $300 billion annually in climate financing by 2035.

But where exactly all those funds will come from remains hotly debated. At the same time, several major developed countries — most notably, the US — have cut their development aid.

The German government only paid out €4.7 billion from its 2025 budget for international climate financing, down from €6.1 billion in 2024.

"The level of financial support for developing countries today is far from what's needed," said Jan Kowalzig, a climate expert at Oxfam Germany.

Breaking financial promises like this comes at the expense of losing the trust of developing countries and, as a result, the diplomatic power, on which Germany and other industrialized nations rely to shape policy to fight climate change.

Cosima Cassel, who focuses on climate diplomacy at the independent think tank E3G in Berlin, believes Turkey's push to turbocharge electrification during an oil crisis could be successful.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For Cassel, a successful outcome at what are expected to be difficult negotiations in Antalya would reveal actual political will behind the grand agreements.

When it comes to electrification, that means taking the expansion of electricity grids and battery storage capacity seriously — not to mention removing the barriers to further electrification.

This article was originally written in German.

#100. This man refused life-saving treatment because of his religion - days later the rules changed

Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure and his condition deteriorated rapidly

In August this year, 26-year-old Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure while browsing in his local library, causing him to convulse and strike his head against the ground.

He lost consciousness. Later, in hospital, it transpired Isaac had hit his head with such force it had led to a brain haemorrhage.

Treatment was seriously complicated by the fact that, as a Jehovah's Witness, Isaac carried a "No Blood" card, an indication that he would not accept treatment involving any form of blood transfusion because of his religious beliefs.

As a result, a procedure to drain fluid from Isaac's brain did not take place.

But his family, from Waxahachie, Texas, were encouraged by the fact that he regained consciousness and even started to talk again. He was able to confirm that he stood by his "no blood" beliefs.

The family's relief at his apparent improvement was short-lived. Isaac slipped into a coma and his condition worsened rapidly.

A neurosurgeon told Isaac's family the only way to save his life was an emergency operation, but that it was one that required blood transfusion.

His family took the decision to abide by Issac's wishes as a lifelong Witness not to accept such a procedure.

Isaac - who his twin brother Jacob Valdez describes as an intelligent man who loved volunteering and helping the elderly - died on 26 August 2026 when doctors turned off his life support.

Jacob's "no blood" card which is like the one carried in Isaac's wallet

Just 23 days later, the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body announced a seismic change in teaching that would, in effect, have given Isaac the religious green light to have the life-saving treatment he had refused.

The change suddenly means the vast majority of medical procedures using donated blood are no longer prohibited to followers. Now, decisions on whether to accept such treatments are to be left to individuals.

Jacob, already dealing with the death of his twin, was angered at the Jehovah's Witnesses' leadership for not coming to their conclusion sooner. He told BBC News he believes Isaac could well still be alive if they had.

Jacob (right) believes his twin Isaac could still be alive if the change in teaching had come sooner

The original rule on blood transfusions dates back to 1945, towards the very end of a war during which the use of blood transfusions had been increasingly widespread. The leaders of Jehovah's Witnesses announced that their believers were not permitted to accept transfusions.

This was based on their interpretation of Bible verses from both the Old and New Testaments prohibiting the consumption of blood, including one from the Book of Acts that instructs believers to abstain from blood, from food offered to idols, and from things that had been strangled.

So, why has the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body, based in New York state, now decided to change the rules, 81 years after introducing them?

It comes after years of pressure from both within and outside the faith, but that is not how leaders of the faith describe the decision.

"The change is basically the application of Bible principles to modern medicine and its methods," says the Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson, Paul Gillies, who described it as both a fresh look at scripture and at medical advances.

Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson Paul Gillies says the ban on transfusion of whole blood remains but blood components can now be used

We meet Gillies at the Jehovah's Witnesses UK headquarters set in 85 acres of Essex countryside. It is a vast modern complex where hundreds of volunteer followers of the faith live and work without pay.

He insists the basic teaching has not changed and that the transfusion of whole blood is still prohibited. However, for decades, most transfusions have not used whole blood, instead using the specific components of blood that are needed by the patient.

The change is being justified by permitting the use of blood components, like plasma and red blood cells and platelets, in the huge numbers of medical procedures that were previously banned.

Gillies says the Bible references "abstaining from blood" but not "various components that could be extracted from blood".

He says the Governing Body felt it their duty to avoid scriptural "overreach" and only apply what was specifically described in the Bible.

But that explanation has stunned many and led them to ask if it was not a huge overreach 81 years ago to ban blood transfusions in the first place, when transfusions are not specifically mentioned in the bible.

Others are then also asking why it has taken so long to come to this landmark decision, given that transfusions that do not use whole blood have been the norm for decades.

These are questions Jacob asked as soon as the ban was lifted, just days after a memorial service for Isaac.

Jacob, who had already left the Jehovah's Witness faith he was brought up in, texted prominent figures from Isaac's congregation in Texas after Isaac's death. The messages are full of angst, and painful to read.

He asked them to consider "the part you played in encouraging so many people to sacrifice their life for something that was never God's will".

Most did not reply, but one "elder" responded saying he could not understand why Jacob was so angry and fixated on the policy shift.

This all further infuriated Jacob, who particularly wants the faith's decision-makers to be accountable for all the lives lost.

"I want to ask them if they could apologise directly, I would like not to have a deflected answer, just straightforward apology," he says.

It is extremely difficult to say how many Jehovah's Witnesses, among the estimated nine million followers of the faith worldwide, died each year having refused treatment involving blood transfusions. But research in one medical journal in 2016, The Obstetrical & Gynecological Survey, put the figure at around 1,100.

"We're sympathetic to their feelings, and if individuals have lost family members and are concerned about some of these questions, they deserve to be heard compassionately," says Gillies.

But there was little clarity about why the change did not come earlier.

"Why has it taken this time? Well, I guess because it takes time, it doesn't necessarily mean that there is some magic change that's taken place," says Gillies.

And when asked, Gillies deflects calls for the leadership to apologise, saying that many lives had, in fact, been saved because faithful Jehovah's Witnesses had not been exposed to blood contamination scandals.

He also insists that even after the new edict, some Jehovah's Witnesses could still decide they did not want to accept a transfusion, and that may have been the case even if the edict had come years ago.

Indeed, the BBC spoke with Alex Ball, 58, a Jehovah's Witness from West Sussex, who says in 2012 a doctor told him he would not survive the chemotherapy he needed to treat his blood cancer unless he also had a transfusion.

"I wanted to adhere as closely to the scriptures as possible when I was sick, and I think I did that well."

With help from what is called "a Jehovah's Witness Hospital Liaison Committee", Alex found an American consultant who could perform a treatment to boost Alex's blood cells without the need for any products from someone else. His UK consultant agreed to treat him using the new method and it worked.

More than a decade later, Alex says he would make the same decision again, despite the change in blood policy that would have permitted the transfusions he had been told he needed.

It is not just receiving blood which has been an issue.

Elisha Cummings, 50, a Jehovah's Witness from Trinidad and Tobago, was unable to donate blood to her aunt, who died, because of the rules.

Elisha Cummings was advised by an elder not to donate blood to her aunt Eva who died

They also cost her her marriage too, with her husband of 10 years leaving the faith and divorcing her after it came to light that he had had a blood transfusion as a child, something that was looked down upon in her Witness community.

"Since the blood policy change, I have not been myself," she says. "It left me in a sort of depressed mode."

"How many houses did I go to trying to convince people about the blood doctrine? And you're coming now and it's something totally different," Elisha says.

Elisha says her beliefs as a whole have been shaken, yet she feels reluctant to leave the faith, partly because it is what provides her with her community and she fears isolation.

In the past, those who disagreed with "blood doctrine" were ostracised or felt like they needed to leave the faith. Will they now be able to come back?

"Any individual that wants to come back to the faith is warmly welcomed," says Gillies, speaking of those who had left the faith because they had accepted a transfusion for themselves or a relative.

"If they would like to become one of Jehovah's Witnesses again and would like to speak to the elders, then they're very welcome to do so."

The pressure on the Church of England to ditch its slavery reparations plan

We thought Gen Z had started going to church in droves. But the truth is more complicated

#98. India: Delhi chokes on most polluted Diwali eve in 10 years

Delhi was ranked as the world's most polluted major city on the eve of Diwali. Delhi's air pollution is expected to get worse in the coming days. DW has more.

Here's a roundup of headlines from across India on Saturday, November 7, and Sunday, November 8:

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi on Sunday joined security forces deployed in the central state of Chhattisgarh to celebrate Diwali.

The state has long been affected by a Maoist-inspired Naxal insurgency.

Celebrating Diwali with security forces in Chhattisgarh's Bastar, Modi said, "Because of your sacrifices, we have put an end to the terror of Naxalism here."

"This is the first Diwali where every single district in the country is free from Naxalism. This is the first Diwali where crores of our citizens have overcome the fear of Naxalism," he said.

Modi made an indirect reference to the youth protests that took place in the past few weeks demanding the removal of election chief Gyanesh Kumar.

Modi called the protesters "Dimagi naxals" (Intellectual naxals) and said, "While armed Naxalites have been wiped out from the forests, 'Dimagi Naxals' have taken control of several organisations."

"Even today, they are inciting people against the country's Constitution and democracy. We must identify them, defeat them and ensure the security of the nation," he said.

Modi's remarks come after his government announced in March that the country has been freed from Naxal groups after several Maoist leaders and cadres were killed in operations by India's security forces.

Activist Sonam Wangchuk has said he is the subject of a witch-hunt by the Ladakh Lieutenant Governor's office, which he accused of targeting him in a smear campaign for standing with the youth of the country and participating in protests.

"I am being continuously witch-hunted and defamed and our schools are being harassed in every possible way by the LG administration in Ladakh," said Wangchuk, who is a member of Leh Apex Body (LAB), a representative body of the people of Ladakh.

In a video message on Saturday to Indian President Droupadi Murmu, Wangchuk asked her to intervene, saying, "Madam President, for the last six years. We don't have rights like in the rest of the country or democracy."

In 2019, Ladakh lost the special status it shared with the former state of Jammu and Kashmir under Article 370 and became a separate Union Territory. The change meant that the Indian central government in New Delhi now directly governs the Himalayan region.

Since 2019, several political and civil society groups in Ladakh have been demanding greater autonomy and, in many cases, statehood.

Wangchuk said two social media pages, Ladakh Lens 1 and Ladakh Vision 1, surfaced on October 14 and that the former was allegedly created to defame him through "unfounded allegations," while the latter was aimed at "praising the Lieutenant Governor's work" in Ladakh.

The Ladakh administration has not responded to the allegations so far.

Wangchuk also accused the Ladakh administration of targeting institutes like his that have "earned national and international repute."

Earlier this month, the Ladakh administration directed the Himalayan Institute of Alternative Learning (HIAL), led by Wangchuk and his wife Gitanjali J. Angmo, to halt activities at its Phyang-based school until the Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh High Court issues further notice.

Wangchuk has questioned the approvals being demanded by the administration.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Delhi awoke Sunday morning enveloped in smog with poor visibility after the previous night saw the worst air quality for Diwali eve in 10 years.

India's Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) said the Indian capital had a "very poor" air quality index (AQI) of 362 on Saturday. The worst polluted pre-Diwali day was in 2016, when the AQI hit 404 on October 29, according to the CPCB.

The air quality is forecast to turn "severe" on Sunday, with Diwali celebrations adding to the pollution.

Swiss monitor IQAir, meanwhile, ranked Delhi as the world's worst polluted major city on Saturday.

While vehicular emissions and stubble burning in neighboring states of Punjab and Haryana are major contributors to air pollution, Diwali celebrations, which involve large-scale burning of firecrackers, add to the pollution in the city.

The government had already ordered schools in and ​around Delhi to offer online classes, and ​encouraged people to work from home ahead of ⁠the Hindu festival of light.

Hello, we are back again with all the news from India.

Diwali celebrations are in full swing, with cities lit up by colourful lights and social media feeds awash with pictures of people dressed up for glamorous Diwali parties.

In the capital, however, like every year, the toxic air continues to dampen the mood. This year, Delhi saw the worst air on Diwali eve in 10 years.

Up in the north, activist Sonam Wangchuk has alleged that he was being continuously witch-hunted by the Ladakh administration.

Stay tuned as we bring you more updates from India through the day.

Indian Prime Minister Nardendra Modi extended Diwali greeting to the nation in a video on X.

He said he would celebrate the festival with soldiers, as he does every year.

Sanitation workers in state capital Bhopal ended their indefinite strike after three days following late-night talks with Chief Minister Mohan Yadav, according to Indian news agency Press Trust of India.

According to the sanitation workers' union, the breakthrough came after the state government agreed to initiate the regularization process for workers employed for 25 days a month, a move which will allow a pay hike.

According to local media, the sanitation workers had four major demands: a pay rise, compassionate appointments, regularization of jobs and an end to the short-term gig worker contract system. Their primary demand is an increase in the monthly wage from Rs 10,500 (about $111) to Rs 25,000.

The protest had left Bhopal, which was named the cleanest state capital in India in 2023, littered with heaps of uncollected garbage.

The workers claimed that more than 100,000 workers participated in the agitation across Madhya Pradesh.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite years of government interventions, emergency restrictions and public outrage, Delhi continues to battle dangerously polluted air every winter. Why isn't there a lasting solution to a predictable crisis?

Learn more about the persistent pollution issues in India's capital in this report by DW's Shakeel Sobhan.

One of India’s most successful fast bowlers, Mohammed Shami, has announced his retirement from Test cricket.

The move brings his 13-year career to an end, but he will continue to play white ball formats.

The 36-year-old cricketer announced in an Instagram video that he was ending his career in whites.

"I am bidding farewell to the white jersey, but never to the memories associated with it. When I wore India's Test jersey for the first time, I had only one dream — to do something for my country. Since then, every wicket, every struggle, every injury, and every comeback has taught me something," Shami said.

Shami made his Test debut against the West Indies at the Eden Gardens on November 6, 2013. He finished with 229 wickets at an average of 27.71, including six five-wicket hauls.

His international red ball journey spans 229 wickets from 64 matches, making him the sixth most successful pacer in Tests for the country behind Kapil Dev (434), Zaheer Khan, Ishant Sharma (both 311), Javagal Srinath (236), Jasprit Bumrah (234).

Shami last played for India in Tests in the World Test Championship final against Australia at The Oval in 2023.

Since then, injuries have disrupted his career, but he remained an important part of India's white-ball setup.

Shami clarified that his retirement would not mark the end of his playing career. He said he would continue to play white-ball cricket and could also turn out in four-day matches occasionally.

Social media platform X said that police orders it received to remove online accounts of two of India's most ​prominent free speech groups were fake.

Lawyers representing X told the court that someone impersonating Delhi police had sent the emails.

X received orders through its online reporting form in the name of a ​senior Delhi police officer seeking removal of the online accounts of free speech groups, the Internet Freedom Foundation (IFF) and the Software Freedom Law Centre, ⁠India (SFLC.in).

The orders claimed the groups had defamed the Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh, parent organization of Prime Minister Narendra Modi's Hindu nationalist Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP).

After an internal investigation, the social media company's lawyers on Friday told the Delhi High Court that, "It ​turns out this was somebody playing an impostor, somebody impersonating Delhi police who has sent this."

The orders had sparked an uproar on social media this week and were challenged for their legality by Amnesty and India's main opposition party.

The Supreme Court of India on Friday said that vehicular pollution is a major contributor to the deteriorating air quality in Delhi and questioned the effectiveness of the government's measures to curb it.

The bench, comprising Chief Justice of India (CJI) Surya Kant and Justices Joymalya Bagchi and V Mohana, opened the proceedings with a jibe saying, "I hope you all are enjoying the weather in Delhi."

This week, Delhi's Air Quality Index (AQI) has remained above the "hazardous" 300 mark.

Air quality in the capital regularly plummets at the outset of winter, as weather conditions help trap pollutants.

"A major cause of the pollution is vehicular pollution. Look at the roads in Delhi, look at the roads connecting Delhi to the NCR (the National Capital Region)… the kind of bumper-to-bumper traffic… that's the major cause," the CJI said.

The court examined the steps taken by the government to control the rising levels of air pollution.

Expressing concerns over the failure of measures to curb air pollution in Delhi over the years, the court said, "To think we can achieve air quality of less than 100 AQI in Delhi during winters is utopian when 'very poor' and 'severe' have become the new normal."

On stubble burning, the court said farmers should be given economic incentives and practical alternatives.

The court also encouraged responsible celebrations during festivals, particularly Diwali, where firecrackers being set off adds to the air pollution.

"Why don't we revert to our original customs?" the CJI said, adding celebrations should be accompanied by a sense of responsibility towards the environment.

#97. Global stocks fall amid ‘big worries over AI’ after Democrats’ midterms triumph

Asian and European markets follow US tech rout with many analysts predicting more waves of selling to come

European and Asian stock markets fell sharply on Friday after the Democrats’ decisive victory in the midterms triggered a rout in US tech stocks a day earlier.

A sell-off on Wall Street on Thursday was followed by sharp falls in Asian stock markets on Friday. The investor sentiment then reached London, where the market was, at one stage, poised for its worst day since March.

The FTSE 100 index, which tracks blue-chip shares listed in London, was down as much as 254 points, or 2.35%, at 10,528 points during a wave of selling in morning trading. But by the close of the day it had clawed back some of its losses to be down 1%, helped by a rise in US stocks when Wall Street reopened.

Germany’s Dax index fell by 2%, France’s Cac 40 dropped by 0.8% and Spain’s Ibex lost 0.6%.

Susannah Streeter, the chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said: “The tech sell-off has intensified, with the pain of the brutal sell-off on Wall Street carried straight through to Asia and knocking sentiment in Europe.

“Big worries are colliding – the build-out of datacentres looks set to slow, which risks shredding the spending spree underpinning the boom, right at the time that investors have become super nervous about returns on this massive investment.”

However, there were signs of easing jitters on Wall Street on Friday, where tech stocks rose, lifting the S&P 500 share index by 0.3% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq closed up 1% – which only recovered a portion of their previous day’s losses.

That followed a stronger-than-expected employment report that showed the US economy added 165,000 new jobs in October, pulling the unemployment rate down to 4.1%.

On Thursday, the Nasdaq share index had its worst day in 19 months after slumping 5.5%.

Chip stocks led the slump on Thursday, with the memory maker SanDisk tumbling by 22% and the hard drive maker Western Digital falling by 17%. Wall Street’s “fear index”, the Vix, jumped by more than 60% to its highest levels since the early weeks of the Iran war, in a sign of rising market volatility.

The heavy falls continued in Asia on Friday, with Japan’s Nikkei 225 index sinking 6% and South Korea’s Kospi index tumbling more than 6.5%.

“It’s actually a bit of a disaster,” said Christopher Forbes, the head of Asia and Middle East at CMC Markets.

“[Clients] were long index, long stocks, and they all got carried out. All night, all day – we had client margin calls, client complaints … because it’s a very emotional thing, right? People are wedded to their positions and views on a stock or outcome, and you’re telling them: ‘Look, you’ve just lost money.’”

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said: “Yesterday’s [Thursday’s] brutal session marked a dramatic turnaround from Wednesday, but with AI fears still prominent there is a risk that there is much more selling to come.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

“Investors remain on watch for a potential resumption of the Iran conflict, and are nervously watching the oil price after its slump earlier in the week. All in all it remains a very febrile atmosphere.”

However, despite the sharp losses suffered on global stock markets around the world, most indices are still up for the year. The Nasdaq Composite index remains up 6.35% since the start of January – even though it is more than 10% below its peak.

The FTSE 100 has gained almost 7.5% in 2026 – but it is now more than 300 points below the record high set in July.

Mohit Kumar, an economist at the investment bank Jefferies, said that while he did not expect a quick rebound from Thursday’s losses in the US, he also did not believe it marked the “start of the unwind of the AI trade”.

He said: “AI investment has been a key driver of US growth and we do not see the Democrats as giving up on this growth engine. Datacentres and AI infrastructure also create jobs and Democrats would be reluctant to let those jobs move on to other states or outside the country.”

Stock market tremors have also hit the London Metal Exchange, with copper poised for its biggest weekly fall since March.

Benchmark three-month copper, a key metal used in the semiconductor, electronics and automotive industry, has dropped 0.61%, leaving it positioned for a 2.79% fall this week.

Craig Lang, a principal analyst at the consultancy CRU, said the price of copper was increasingly influenced by the AI market and “sentiment towards datacentre expansion”.

#95. Germans wrestle with what to do with Hitler's destroyed Eagle's Nest retreat

Fire engulfed Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat in Bavaria last month

Perched on a ridge high up in the spectacular scenery of the German Alps lie the burned-out ruins of a mountain house called The Eagle's Nest, which is now at the heart of an intense debate about Germany's Nazi past.

Known in German as Kehlsteinhaus, the retreat was built by the Nazis in 1938, and frequented by Adolf Hitler and other high-ranking Nazis and their guests. After World War Two it was classified as a historical monument recalling the Nazi era.

Last month it was gutted by fire in a suspected arson attack, and some believe it should be completely destroyed, the site returned to nature and all memory of its Nazi past forgotten.

But others want it either partially or completely rebuilt, wary of the risk of turning it into a site for Neo-Nazi pilgrimage at a time when Germany is witnessing the resurgence of the far-right.

Germany has an "everlasting responsibility to communicate, to deal with our Nazi past", said Isabel Heinemann, professor of contemporary history at Munich's Ludwig-Maximilians-UniversitÃ¤t (LMU).

But rebuilding the site completely, she warned, would mean putting "highly ideologised or highly ideological architecture" back in place.

Adolf Hitler used the mountain retreat to welcome guests

Before it was burned down during the evening of 23 October, the Eagle's Nest had been a popular tourist attraction, owned by the state of Bavaria and operating for many years as a restaurant.

Visitors would take a bus and then ride the original brass and mirror-panelled lift through the mountain to the Kehlsteinhaus to savour the magnificent views above the town of Berchtesgaden.

Once there, they could eat sausages in the beer garden on the Obersalzberg at 1,834m (6,017ft) above sea level, overlooking the Alps.

Unlike Hitler's nearby summer residence, the Berghof, which was bombed in World War Two and demolished in 1952, the Eagle's Nest survived the war.

Tens of thousands of tourists visited each season, providing an important source of income to the region.

In the 1990s, amid concerns that the site did not do enough to confront the crimes of the Nazi past, a museum, the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, was opened close by.

Before it was burned down, the Kehlsteinhaus or Eagle's Nest had long been a tourist destination in Bavaria

Around 15 years ago, a photo exhibit was opened in the Kehlsteinhaus about its history.

Although the Eagle's Nest itself was "largely destroyed" by the fire, according to police, the documentation centre survived unscathed.

The authorities have not commented on a possible motive, but a 33-year-old man with a history of mental illness has been detained in a secure psychiatric hospital on suspicion of arson.

Man arrested after fire at Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat

Some feel this is the moment to allow the site to fade into history.

"It's been torched now. It's been destroyed. I think they should just clear away the rubble and leave the mountain as it should be in its natural state," said Prof Sir Richard J. Evans, a specialist in Nazi Germany at the University of Cambridge.

That has happened with "quite a few other Nazi sites", he argued, including the bunker in Berlin where Hitler killed himself: "The bunker was filled in with concrete and a children's playground put on the top of it on the ground."

The Kehlsteinhaus was just "a Nazi vanity project" since romanticised by the name Eagle's Nest, he said.

Police say the old Nazi retreat was largely destroyed by last month's fire

But Bavaria's authorities take a different view about the site's future.

Both the state's interior minister, Joachim Herrmann, and its minister for the arts, Markus Blume, have spoken out in favour of rebuilding the Kehlsteinhaus.

"We can hardly leave a ruin standing there," Herrmann said.

Sven Keller, head of the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, believes it is important not to leave the site in ruins, as that could encourage Neo-Nazi pilgrims, who would be able to hike up there completely undisturbed.

"We have quite some experience at Obersalzberg with what happens if we clear those sites. Hitler's Berghof was demolished in the 1950s, and in our experience, this does not remove such sites from the mental maps of people," he told the BBC's Today programme.

Nor does he believe "a one-on-one reconstruction" of the site is possible, and Blume has made clear on Bavarian radio that "it should not and must not cost the taxpayer anything".

After Hitler's downfall, US soldiers captured the mountain-top retreat and are seen relaxing here on 4 May 1945

Herrmann said they would look for a sensible solution in consultation with historians, given the historically complex circumstances surrounding the building.

Heinemann, who also heads the Leibniz Institute for Contemporary History, believes full-scale reconstruction should be "out of the question".

"I rather think we can document the state left by the fire and also document the historical remains of the Kehlsteinhaus, and then reconstruct parts in a modern design, to make clear the different layers of time that can be seen in this building," she told the BBC.

Germany and Austria have long struggled to deal with the fraught legacy of buildings and sites connected to the Nazi era.

With the recent rise of the far-right AfD and the Austrian Freedom Party, the debate over Erinnerungskultur (Culture of Remembrance) has taken on a new urgency.

A row broke out recently in Germany when AfD and conservative CDU councillors in a town in Saxony decided to ban "stumbling stones" memorials - embedded small brass plaques in the streets - to victims of the Nazis.

In Austria over the summer, a police station was opened in the house where Hitler was born in Braunau am Inn. It followed years of controversy about how the house should be used.

The Austrian government said the police presence would help prevent neo-Nazi pilgrims.

But others had wanted the house to become a centre of responsibility, actively confronting the Nazi past.

As one resident of Braunau am Inn told me, it was a delicate balance between how much to remember and how much to forget.
