#73. Flávio Bolsonaro battles to remain relevant as he speaks at rightwing conference at Mar-a-Lago

Far-right politician addresses Conservative Political Action Conference and makes unsubstantiated claims about election fraud

Flávio Bolsonaro, the far-right politician who lost Brazil’s presidential election last month, has insisted his defeat is not “the end for Brazil” as he battles to maintain political relevance after fleeing to the US.

Bolsonaro absconded early last week shortly after losing the 25 October election to the leftwing incumbent Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva by more than 4m votes.

On Thursday night Bolsonaro made his first major public appearance since arriving in the US, speaking at a rightwing gathering at Donald Trump’s Mar-a-Lago property where he claimed “great things” were still to come.

“I know this wasn’t the end for Brazil and I know it wasn’t the end for America,” Bolsonaro, 45, told a gala dinner at the Conservative Political Action Conference (CPAC)’s four-day “gathering of visionaries” event, where the former British prime minister Liz Truss is also due to speak.

“Even when what seems like the end isn’t what we’d hoped for, we must thank God and have even more faith that this isn’t yet the end,” he added. “No one shuts the door that he opens – and the door he shuts, no one opens.”

Earlier, in his first post-election interview, Bolsonaro made a series of unsubstantiated claims about election fraud and insisted he received “help from absolutely no one” to flee.

“It was all me,” Bolsonaro told the New York Times, contradicting reports that he was helped by the Trump adviser Jason Miller and Santiago Caputo, a close aide to Argentina’s rightwing president, Javier Milei.

He said he would “take to the grave” the full story of how he had fled Brazil, where he faces federal police investigations for money laundering and corruption. Bolsonaro is a member of congress who has held elected office for more than 20 years but he will lose parliamentary immunity early next year when his term ends, meaning he could then be prosecuted.

Brazilian analysts agree that by skipping the country after an election in which he received 59m votes, Bolsonaro, the son of jailed former president Jair Bolsonaro, abdicated leadership of the opposition and sabotaged his political future.

Thomas Traumann, an author and pundit for the TV network GloboNews, said: “Flávio was foolish enough to throw away the leadership of the opposition that he could have exercised had he stayed in Brazil. But the [Bolsonaro] family is still a player.”

Flávio Bolsonaro’s brother, the former congressman Eduardo Bolsonaro, also lives in self-imposed exile in the US and will have to serve a four-year sentence for coercion if he returns to Brazil. Four other members of the Bolsonaro family were elected to congress in last month’s election, including their brothers Carlos and Jair Renan and stepmother, Michelle Bolsonaro.

Traumann believed it was not inconceivable that Jair Bolsonaro would attempt a political comeback if he managed to negotiate his way out of his 27-year prison sentence for plotting a coup after losing the 2022 election to Lula. However, as things stand, the 71-year-old is banned from running for office until 2060 and must live under house arrest.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Many doubt the Bolsonaro family can continue to control Bolsonarismo, the populist political movement that takes their name, with two key members in exile and one imprisoned.

“[The movement] is mortally wounded because of its mistakes and its crimes,” the political scientist Sérgio Abranches told the newspaper O Globo this week.

Abranches said democratic rightwing politicians now had a chance to “detach” themselves from the Bolsonaros’ anti-democratic movement and break off in a new direction before the next presidential election in 2030.

Flávio Bolsonaro told the New York Times he would “obviously” be a candidate in future elections but said his future lay in the US “at least for now”.

It is unclear where Flávio Bolsonaro, who has been granted a US visa by Trump, will live. His brother Eduardo set up home in Southlake, Texas, after leaving Brazil last year – although some expect him to move after the traditionally conservative state elected the Democrat Gina Hinojosa as governor in this week’s midterms.

In a video posted on CPAC’s social networks before Thursday’s event, Eduardo Bolsonaro played down the risk posed by the “blue tsunami” to their ability to stay in the US. “They [Democrats] will try [to make trouble for us] – but it’s not up to Congress … to revoke visas or citizenships,” he said.

Likening their plight to that of a female asylum seeker fleeing Iran for fear of being killed after refusing to wear a hijab, he added: “I am 100% legal – as is my brother and his family.”

#71. Kenyan doomsday cult leader given life sentence for murdering 191 people

Paul Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", the judge said last month

A Kenyan court has sentenced the leader of a doomsday cult, Paul Mackenzie, to 200 years in prison for murdering 191 people, all but one of them children.

Alongside 28 others, Mackenzie was found guilty of the murders for instructing members of his cult to starve their children to death so they could "meet Jesus".

Justice Diana Mochache said Mackenzie bore the "primary criminal culpability" for ordering his followers to fast to death, in one of the world's deadliest episodes of cult activity.

"The sentence is appropriate and just, to send an unmistakable message that such atrocities warrant the maximum penalties allowed by law," the judge added.

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves in Shakahola forest near the Kenyan coast. The sprawling investigation into the cases shocked Kenya and drew international attention.

The court sentenced Mackenzie to 100 years in prison for homicide and another 100 years for offences related to starvation.

The sentences within each category will run concurrently, but the two sets will run consecutively, bringing his total prison term to 200 years.

Mackenzie also faces separate manslaughter and terrorism charges, which he denies.

Prosecutors had sought the death penalty, arguing that the scale and circumstances of the killings warranted the harshest punishment.

However, Justice Mochache declined to impose the death penalty, despite ruling that the case met the threshold for its consideration.

"Nevertheless, the court declines the invitation to impose the death penalty on any of the victims," she said, adding that capital punishment would eliminate any prospect of rehabilitation and disengagement. The death sentence has not been carried out in Kenya since 1987.

Mackenzie was tried alongside 28 accomplices, including guards who enforced his fasting orders and gravediggers who helped conceal the victims' bodies in mass graves.

They received varying prison sentences of up to 160 years.

Justice Mochache described the killings as "cold and calculated", saying the perpetrators had shown no remorse and had even declared their willingness to fast to death themselves.

Before the hearing began on Friday morning, Francis Wanje, who lost eight relatives including two grandchildren in Shakahola forest, told the BBC's Newsday programme that he was "hoping for... a sentence which will match his [Mackenzie's] deeds in the forest".

Mackenzie and the co-accused had asked for leniency from the court.

Before their conviction on 19 October, they had all pleaded not guilty and repeatedly insisted that nobody was forced to die.

More than 400 bodies were dug up in Shakahola forest

Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", Justice Mochache said as she delivered her eight-hour judgement last month.

Those who opposed Mackenzie's teachings were beaten to death.

One of his co-accused said he had told his followers that anyone who "resisted the directive to fast must be forced to go to heaven through kicks and blows".

Burials inside the forest where the cult was based were called "weddings" and those who died "were celebrated as having wedded with Jesus" the judge said.

The judge found that the deaths arose from an organised system, including religious teachings and directives that compelled followers to follow the deadly fasting.

Post-mortem results showed the cause of deaths to be starvation for most victims, while others died from suffocation, strangulation or head injuries.

The cause of death could not be determined for some, with their bodies having decomposed, while others died soon after their rescue as they were so emaciated.

The Shakahola deaths have shone a spotlight on the lack of oversight of rogue religious activities and how they could have carried on for so long.

The gravity of the case prompted President William Ruto to set up a commission of inquiry, although this was later declared unconstitutional.

Investigations by the state-funded Kenya National Commission on Human Rights (KNCHR) revealed that local administration and law enforcement officials had repeatedly turned a blind eye to the cult's activities.

"We expected at that time the police would take action immediately to go and rescue the other members there, but it didn't happen," said Wanje, who reported the case to the police in March 2023.

Wanje said a relative of his in the police force and a former cult participant had managed to save his grandson at the time. The child told the police what had happened and showed them the grave of his siblings.

"It was [supposed to be] his last day.. they [would have killed] my grandson."

The cult leader had previously been arrested and later released for lack of evidence.

Mackenzie established Good News International Ministries in 2003.

Over the next 16 years, the church grew in popularity, drawing thousands of followers in its branches across Kenya.

The church ran a television station called Times TV which would broadcast his sermons from his church in Malindi.

His End Days sermons included conspiracy theories that described education as "evil", healthcare as "against the doctrine" and digital identity cards as "the mark of the devil".

The main church shut down in 2019 and Mackenzie moved to Shakahola forest, encouraging his devotees to follow him.

While the conclusion of the murder cases may bring a sense of justice for the victims and their families, the convictions only account for 191 victims, while more than 400 bodies were exhumed from the forest.

#69. Spain's rail authorities face European fraud probe after Urda rail disaster

Last month's train crash in which 69 people died was the second deadly accident on the same high-speed line this year

European Union prosecutors have begun investigating the possible misuse of EU funds meant for upgrading the Spanish railway line where a train crash killed 69 people last week.

Prosecutors backed by a special unit of Spain's Guardia Civil security force said they were conducting the inquiry at the offices of Adif rail infrastructure authority and rail safety agency AESF.

The 28 October accident on the Madrid-Seville high-speed line was one of the worst railway disasters in Europe this century.

It is the second EU fraud inquiry opened into Spain's railways this year, after 46 people were killed on the same line at Adamuz in January.

Last week, a train operated by state-owned operator Renfe ploughed into the back of a stationary train belonging to private Spanish company Iryo outside the village of Urda, around 130km (80 miles) south of Madrid.

The LZB communication system that monitors the position of trains had gone down, and the Renfe train had been given the all clear to start running at normal speed, even though the Iryo train ahead of it had been unable to restart.

The two accidents nine months apart on the same line have claimed 115 lives and dozens more injuries.

Shock of rail tragedy gives way to sorrow and outrage in politically divided Spain

Spanish transport minister resigns days after deadly train crash

The European Public Prosecutor's Office (EPPO) said on Friday it had begun conducting investigative measures at Spain's rail authorities "after the opening of an investigation into possible fraud affecting EU funds, external allocated to the renovation and upgrading of safety, signalling and communications systems on the Madrid-Seville high-speed railway line".

The EPPO highlighted high public interest in the case, but said no further details would be made public.

As part of the process, a Spanish judge assigned to the case has to ensure the investigation complies with legal safeguards, including authorising any measures that might involve house searches or phone-tapping.

Spanish Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez (C) faces parliamentary elections on 29 November

The Urda disaster has damaged the Socialist-led government of Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez ahead of a 29 November general election and the party is trailing the conservative People's Party (PP) in most opinion polls.

Close SÃ¡nchez ally Ãscar Puente resigned this week as transport minister, after seeing two deadly accidents on his watch this year.

The PP and far-right Vox say Puente's resignation is not enough and they have demanded that SÃ¡nchez resign too.

Many people in Spain have now become wary of taking the train, with reports of widespread cancellations and empty station platforms.

A funeral for the 69 victims at Urda will take place on Tuesday at Toledo cathedral, in the same province that the disaster happened.

Puente's successor, Carlos Cuerpo, has vowed to take all necessary measures to "restore the confidence of Spaniards in our high-speed rail".

Cuerpo met survivors of January's Adamuz disaster on Friday and they handed him a list of 11 demands for improving safety on Spain's railways.

#67. Sebastián Lelio, director of A Fantastic Woman and Gloria, dies aged 52

The Chilean film-maker, whose films starred Florence Pugh, Julianne Moore and Rachel Weisz, died at his home in Santiago

Chilean film-maker Sebastián Lelio has died at the age of 52.

He was best known for the Oscar-winning trans drama A Fantastic Woman and his English-language films included Gloria Bell and The Wonder.

His partner Amalia Kassai announced his death on Instagram without specifying the cause of death but telling followers, in Spanish, that it was a painful time for his family. Chilean media reports that he died at his home in Las Condes, Santiago.

Lelio was born in Argentina but moved to Chile at the age of two. He studied journalism for one year before graduating from film school and then released 9/11 documentary Cero in 2003. His first narrative film The Sacred Family premiered at the San Sebastián film festival in 2005.

His follow-up, 2009’s Christmas, premiered at Cannes but it was 2013’s Gloria that provided him with a major international breakthrough. The drama, about a woman in her late 50s who starts dating again, premiered at Berlin and won its lead star Paulina García an acting award at the festival.

Lelio followed it up with A Fantastic Woman, a drama about the life of a trans woman, another breakout that became the first Chilean film to win the Oscar for best international feature. In a five-star review, the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw called it “a sublime study in the exalted ordeal of grief”.

“When we released it in 2017, a gender identity bill had been dormant in congress for seven years,” he said in a Guardian interview in 2018. “Somehow, the film’s release, and the year in which it became globally known, helped to reactivate the discussion in Chile.”

He made his English language debut with London-set drama Disobedience which told a queer love story between women played by Rachel Weisz and Rachel McAdams. It was nominated for five British independent film awards.

Lelio then remade Gloria as Gloria Bell, then set in America and starring Julianne Moore in the lead role. He followed it with a critically acclaimed adaptation of Emma Donoghue’s novel The Wonder starring Florence Pugh.

“I love working with actors, and need to know they trust me and can explore, get lost, be foolish or brave, and I’ll take care of them,” he said to the Guardian. “I love working on set – it is where the sparkle is.”

His final film was 2025’s The Wave, a musical protest drama that premiered at Cannes.

“Making a film is like a gift you leave somewhere – you then hide and see what happens,” he said.

#62. New wave of bomb threats hit European airlines

European airlines have again been hit with a new wave of bomb threats - in the third such incident this week.

On Friday multiple flights to and from Lisbon, Rome, Prague and Sofia were targeted and the threats saw three planes escorted by Hungarian fighter jets, authorities said.

It also saw a number of high-profile media organisations, including Ireland's public broadcaster RTÃ, evacuated after threats were made against them - although there has been no concrete links between the messages against airlines and the broadcasters.

The disruption comes after nearly 30 aircraft and several airports across Europe were impacted by threats earlier this week, in what the UK called a "malicious hoax".

Russian extremist group denies sending hoax bomb threats

A Ryanair flight scheduled to leave Lisbon for Rome on Friday was targeted with a threat, the airline said, while at least 11 flights were targeted in the Czech Republic and Bulgaria, according to AFP.

France also reported at least four of its flights were targeted by threats, however they all landed safely.

No explosives have been found on any planes as of Friday.

As airlines were dealing with this latest wave of threats, several media outlets, including RTÃ, were evacuated after similar warnings were made against them.

Staff at the Irish Independent newspaper and the Dublin offices of radio stations NewstalkFM and TodayFM, were also advised to leave their buildings and UK broadcaster Sky said it also received a security threat.

In a statement, gardaÃ­ (Irish police) said they were "aware of a threat made against media organisations here and in Europe".

Armed gardaÃ­ were deployed to RTÃ'S Dublin headquarter after a bomb threat

While no link has been established between the threats against airlines and media outlets, the Irish government has warned it is a "likely coordinated campaign of disruption".

In a statement, it said: "Hoax threats such as these cause fear, distress and disruption to people going about their daily lives and activities; they achieve no positive purpose whatsoever. It is not known yet who is responsible for these threats, whether it is individuals or a centralised or co-ordinated effort.

Irish Prime Minister MicheÃ¡l Martin described the situation as "worrying" and added his government's first priority was the safety of the Irish public.

"Yesterday, there appeared to be coordinated efforts to disrupt air travel," he said.

Martin told RTÃ it was not yet known if the threats had come from individuals or if it was a "coordinated attack" but said they were being taken seriously.

Ireland is scheduled to host two high-profile European Union meetings in Dublin next week.

Broadcaster Sky confirmed it had received a security threat, hours after RTÃ had evacuated its building.

The broadcaster, which is owned by Comcast, said in a statement read by a presenter on its news channel that the broadcaster had "carried out a security assessment and reported the matter to the relevant authorities".

"The safety and security of our colleagues is our primary concern, and we are working closely with authorities to ensure our people and operations remain secure."

Friday's disruption comes after the Russian extremist group "Male State", named in this week's hoax bomb threats to Italian airports, denied any link to the false warnings.

Replying to the BBC Russian Service, an account on Telegram - one of several linked to the so-called "Male State" and its founder Vladislav Pozdnyakov - mocked any suggestion the group was involved in emailing the threats.

"What Male State? Where have you seen it? It hasn't existed for many years," the message on the channel reads. "If tomorrow they sign a [threat] letter, say, from Putin, will you then try to reach our president to ask your stupid question?"

#58. Ousted Paraguay president under house arrest after banking crisis leaves 60% of country unable to access accounts

Santiago Peña was detained minutes after he was impeached by Congress, as protesters took to the streets demanding answers after the suspension of the Ueno bank

Paraguay’s rightwing president has been placed under house arrest minutes after he was impeached by Congress, amid a severe crisis that left more than half the country’s population unable to access their bank accounts.

Santiago Peña is being investigated about allegations he used his political influence to favour a bank that expanded exponentially under his government and was suspended this week after irregularities.

The move froze the accounts of 2.9 million people, about 60% of the country’s adult population, who were customers of the bank – Ueno - and who overnight could no longer use their own money even for basic purchases such as bread and water.

Protests spread across the country, some involving violent clashes with police. Impeachment proceedings began on Wednesday before being concluded on Friday, minutes before Peña’s arrest was ordered.

His vice-president, Pedro Alliana, was being sworn in as the country’s new leader while a public prosecutor, accompanied by police outside Peña’s home, told local media the former president had been placed under house arrest.

“It has been established that he is indeed inside his home. He voluntarily allowed officers to enter and made himself available for all procedural purposes,” said prosecutor Elena Fiore Franco.

The public prosecutor’s office had opened an investigation into Peña on Thursday on suspicion of illicit enrichment, money laundering and influence peddling.

Peña, who was one of US president Donald Trump’s main allies in the region, had tried to resign on Thursday in an attempt to retain the special jurisdiction afforded to former presidents – who become senators for life in Paraguay – but lawmakers did not accept his resignation, leaving him subject to the ordinary courts.

Amid rumours he had fled to Argentina, the 47-year-old economist was expected to appear before Congress to present his defence during the impeachment proceedings, but left that to his lawyers and instead continued posting on social media.

Before the impeachment was concluded or the arrest warrant issued, he posted: “I say goodbye with gratitude, while fully recognising the anguish of those who are now waiting for answers,” referring to former Ueno customers who are unable to access their own money.

Soon after taking office, Peña appointed Carlos Carvallo Spalding, a former CEO of Ueno, head of the country’s central bank. Three months later, Spalding – who was by now regulating the company he used to run – allowed Ueno to become a digital bank, which put it on the path to becoming one of Paraguay’s largest banks by deposits in just a few years.

The arrest warrant for Peña also includes Spalding.

Peña himself had about US$1m in shares he bought in 2019 in a company that would become Ueno Holding in 2022, the parent company of the conglomerate that controlled Ueno Bank. Even after becoming president, he only disposed of the shares in April 2025. He said he had bought them purely as an “investor”.

As the bank aggressively expanded through an easy account-opening system and by promising returns far above those offered by the rest of the market, it also managed to bring public pension funds controlled by Peña’s administration into its portfolio.

“Because Ueno had the blessing of the presidency, most of the pension funds close to the government went to deposit their money in the bank,” said political analyst Hugo Duarte. An estimated $800m to $860m in state and pension funds were invested in Ueno.

As pressure mounted and retail sales fell by as much as 80% because much of the population could not access their own money, Peña lost the support of his own party.

During the impeachment session, opposition senator Rafael Filizzola said Paraguay was facing “one of the biggest corruption scandals since the return of democracy” and the “biggest fraud carried out from political power in the democratic era”.

#57. AI stocks climb after trillions wiped from market values

Shares in artificial intelligence and technology firms have recovered slightly from an enormous sell-off that wiped trillions of dollars off stock values.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq stock index closed in positive territory following a market rout that began in the US on Thursday before spilling over to Asian and European markets into Friday.

Investors had been selling off tech shares in part due to concerns the Democrat victory in the midterm elections would mean slower artificial intelligence development.

However the latest US employment figures added to a mixed picture for American markets on Friday morning, with an unexpectedly strong 165,000 jobs added to the economy in September.

On Thursday, businesses closely linked to artificial intelligence development took a hammering on stock markets, particularly chip and semiconductor makers.

But the Nasdaq Composite bounced back slightly on Friday, ending the day up 1.1%, partly reversing some of its 5.5% fall.

The S&P 500, which tracks the share prices of the largest publicly-traded US companies, closed up 0.3%, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was flat through the day before ending slightly down 0.4%.

Companies that lost hundreds of billions in market value recovered some ground on Friday, including Nvidia, SpaceX and Tesla.

However, stock markets in Asia and Europe fared worse.

The Kospi in South Korea closed down 6% on Friday, with shares in chipmaker SK Hynix plunging more than 10%. European markets also fell, with the main indexes in Germany, France and Spain ending the day down.

In the UK, the FTSE 100, which tracks the largest companies on the London Stock Exchange, declined 1.01%.

In addition to the recent market volatility, UK traders were also digesting the latest Bank of England decision to raise interest rates and the signal that another rate rise could be on the cards to curb rising prices.

The market fluctuations also come after the sweeping Democrat victory in the midterm elections.

A number of Democrats campaigned for new rules and regulations for artificial intelligence (AI) and data centres, and the party has since won control of both houses of congress as well as key contests in states that have been part of the AI data centre race.

US President Donald Trump on Friday reiterated his supported for what he calls "super intelligence", saying only "foolish leadership" would "say no to the riches they bring".

However, there have also been concerns over whether the enormous amounts of money being spent on AI will pay off.

Russ Shaw, founder of global tech advocacy firm Tech London Advocates, said the election result was sending a message that a rethink was needed on AI.

"The incoming Congress is going to really focus on AI in a very dramatic way," he told the BBC's Business Today.

"I'm a big fan of AI, but I'm watching what these companies are doing, and they're not communicating effectively at all, particularly in the US, about what the benefits of AI are all about."

Pierre BaquÃ©, chief executive of AI platform Neural Concept, said the share movements were a "necessary market correction" that reflected a legitimate concern about the amount of money being poured into AI infrastructure.

"The industry must move beyond unchecked infrastructure investment and demonstrate greater financial discipline," he said.

"We are entering a critical transition phase, a market purgatory where raw tech must prove its widespread economic value."

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said it had been a brutal week for markets, and the key question was whether the jump in the S&P 500 and Nasdaq would be sustained.

"Once investors have had the weekend to catch their breath, next week could give a clearer indication of whether the tech trade is going down the pan or fighting back."

Could the US midterms put a pin in the AI bubble?

Elon Musk appears to signal the end of the road for Cybertruck

As well as the fallout of the midterms and the plunge in artificial intelligence stocks, US traders will also be digesting the latest jobs figures.

And those numbers were better than expected: US employers added 165,000 new jobs in October, well above the average monthly increase and more than double what analysts had expected.

Overall the unemployment rate, which counts the number of people who are out of work but actively looking for a job, eased slightly to 4.1% from 4.2% in September.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) said the unemployment rate has remained in the narrow range of 4.1% to 4.3% since March.

Neil Birrell, chief investment officer at investment firm Premier Miton, said while the jobs figures may feel like second billing to the market movements they were still important.

"The US economy is running hard which may mean the risks the markets are worrying about at the moment are not such a risk to the real economy as is being feared," he said.

#53. Texas mayor instructs police to limit co-operation with ICE

John Whitmire is also calling on Congress to change immigration laws

The mayor of Houston, Texas, has directed the city's police to limit co-operation with federal immigration officials.

The move by John Whitmire, a Democrat, comes on the heels of stunning victories by the party in Texas during this week's midterm elections. It breaks with policy set by Republican Governor Greg Abbott, who lost his re-election bid.

"I do this to begin a healing process," Whitmire said on Friday, and to "reduce fear and anxiety across our community". He added: "I also do this knowing that elections matter."

There has been months of public outcry in Houston after an Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) officer fatally shot a man who was driving to work.

Houston police chief Noe Diaz said on Friday that officers will now only contact ICE if someone they arrest for a jailable offence also has an administrative ICE warrant, which are issued internally by the Department of Homeland Security (DHS), within which ICE operates.

"For example, if our police officers pull someone over for a minor traffic infraction and they write a citation, that person receives a citation and goes about their day," Diaz said.

Under the previous rules, Houston police officers were required to inform ICE if anyone they were dealing with came up as having one of these warrants attached to their name when they were searched on the National Crime Information Center (NCIC) - a digital database that includes records on wanted persons, stolen property, certain missing people and those arrested and fingerprinted for serious offences.

Whitmire said on Friday that he had directed the Houston Police Department to make the changes through an executive order.

He also called on Congress to change federal immigration rules, saying "ICE needs better training. They need body cameras. They need to be trained in de-escalation."

He added, "But this can only be done in Washington. I can only control what I can control."

Five ways Trump has changed immigration in the US

In response, a spokesperson for the DHS told Houston's NBC affiliate, KPRC 2, in a statement: "We remain disappointed that sanctuary politicians seek to limit cooperation with ICE, as our law enforcement are simply fulfilling their sworn duties to serve and protect our communities."

"Partnerships with law enforcement are critical to having the resources we need to arrest criminal illegal aliens across the country," the spokesperson added.

Another Texas city, Austin, has also begun implementing a similar policy change.

Democrats won both the US Senate and House of Representatives in Tuesday's midterms, taking control of Congress for the rest of Donald Trump's presidency.

Texas, a long-time Republican stronghold, was the site of one of the most stunning upsets in the vote, with the state electing its first Latina governor, Gina Hinojosa, as the first Democrat governor since 1995. It also elected its first Democratic senator since 1988.

Whitmire has previously faced criticism for abiding by immigration enforcement policies under the Trump administration.

He had resisted previous calls to implement similar changes over concerns that the White House could send a surge of immigration officials in response. , external

Abbott has also threatened to take pull millions in funds from cities like Houston, Dallas and Austin unless they allow police to cooperate fully with federal immigration officials.

The BBC has contacted Abbott's office for comment.

Whitmire's announcement comes a week after an ICE officer was charged with aggravated assault with a deadly weapon after he allegedly hit a man with a vehicle in Texas.

Earlier this year, large protests broke out in Houston after an ICE officer fatally shot a man driving to work.

The deceased, Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, a 52-year-old Mexican national who lived in the US as an undocumented immigrant for decades, was not the intended target, officials said after the incident.

#50. Banana producers in Latin America want better pay to cope with effects of climate crisis

Farmers face big drop in production from extreme weather during this year’s super El Niño event

Banana producers in Latin America are calling for better pay to help cope with the impact of the climate crisis, as they face big drops in production resulting from extreme weather during this year’s super El Niño event.

High winds in parts of Colombia, one of the biggest suppliers to the UK market where droughts are predicted in some regions this year, and heavy rainfall in Peru and Ecuador are already affecting production, according to the Fairtrade Foundation ethical trade body. In the Dominican Republic, farmers are expected to face drought conditions, with forecasts of drier and warmer than usual conditions.

Fairtrade research suggests that during the previous El Niño cycle, productivity across its farmers and workers dropped by 13% in the Dominican Republic, 22% in Ecuador, and 6% in Peru compared with the year before. The El Niño event in 1983 – one of the strongest ever recorded – affected 15% of total planted banana area in Ecuador, with a reduction in exports of 36%.

Dr Carley-Jane Stanton, a climate and environment lead at Fairtrade, says this El Niño is expected to lead to more extreme weather, including heavy rainfall and strong winds and “we expect the impacts for producers to be more severe” than in the 23-24 cycle.

Evidence is mounting to fuel those fears. Exports from the Colombian Caribbean region fell 32% to $122m (£91m) between July and September this year, according to the Association of Banana Growers of Magdalena and La Guajira, as reported by the trade publication Fruitnet.

It said that in September alone, shipments were down 42% year on year, with earnings close to halving to $40m from $70m.

“Given the risks threatening the continuity of banana production, we are calling on international markets to foster commercial conditions that recognise actual production costs and contribute to the sector’s sustainability,” the association said.

It said that climate-related productivity losses and a decline in earnings from exports made worse by currency issues was “undermining the economic sustainability of the industry”.

Ever Antonio Flores Muños, a worker at the Agrosiete banana producer in Colombia, told Fairtrade: “Over the past 10 years the climate has changed a lot. It’s been getting much hotter … it’s really intense. Some places have had losses due to the climate, flooding, strong winds. It’s going to affect all of us – our workers, colleagues – everything will be at great risk, and our wages will decrease.”

Fairtrade is calling for legislation in the UK to tackle unfair trading practices, such as low or late payments to farmers and contracts which push risk down the supply chain, so that producers can invest more into improving their crops’ resilience to severe weather and more likelihood of pests and diseases. Worker health is also expected to be affected by more extreme weather.

While some businesses, including Sainsbury’s, the Co-op and Waitrose, have taken voluntary steps by sourcing only Faitrade bananas, which guarantee a minimum price, it says there is more work to do.

Eleanor Harrison, the chief executive of Fairtade, said: “The UK’s banana supply depends on the people growing our bananas having the resources they need to adapt to climate change, and to invest in their future. Fair prices, climate resilience and decent work go hand in hand.”

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

While bananas are becoming harder and more expensive to produce, supermarket prices have remained unchanged for almost 40 years despite rising costs for fertiliser, fuel and labour.

Caoimhe Buckley, the chief corporate affairs officer at the large supplier Fyffes, said: “The long-term sustainability of the banana sector … cannot be separated from purchasing practices. The price of bananas in the supermarket needs to recognise the real and sustainable cost of production, including labour and decent wages, environmental management, human-rights due diligence and the investment necessary to adapt to climate change.”

Research by the Ethical Trading Initiative found that prices paid to suppliers in 2026 did not even cover basic production costs for 66.7% of them.

It said producers need more help to adapt or mitigate climatic changes, which could mean suitable land for banana cultivation could shrink by an average of 61% across Latin America and the Caribbean by 2050, according to research for the Inter-American Development Bank published in 2020.

#48. Meghan's father Thomas Markle under house arrest after child sex abuse material charge

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, has been placed under house detention at his assisted living facility in Florida after being charged over possession of child sex abuse material.

Markle, 82, was arrested in Florida on a federal complaint alleging he held images that show child sex abuse, the US Department of Justice (DoJ) said.

He is charged with one count of possessing indecent images of children, the DoJ said. Markle has previously denied allegations of possessing images of child sex abuse, calling them "sick lies".

In a 30-minute court hearing on Friday he was also ordered to submit to electronic monitoring, but no plea was taken.

At the court hearing, the charges were laid out against him by federal prosecutor Courtenay Derry, according to the BBC's US partner CBS News.

He was brought into court in a wheelchair, escorted by two US Marshals. Markle was wearing black trousers and a black t-shirt.

No plea was taken during the hearing, and an arraignment date in California will be determined in due course, a joint statement from the DoJ and the US Attorney's Office Central California said.

The Duke and Duchess of Sussex have not commented on Markle's arrest.

Markle was arrested after the FBI, acting on warrants, searched storage units in LA registered to him which he had rented since 2008, with the last payment being made in October this year, the DoJ said.

Inside the units the FBI says agents seized about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

FBI director Kash Patel said authorities "moved quickly" when arresting Markle.

"Mr Markle allegedly targeted innocent children and sought to steal their innocence through his criminal actions," Patel said.

He added that the FBI would "use every single resource at our disposal to protect innocent kids â any predator who targets children will have zero safe haven in this country or anywhere they try to hide".

Thomas Markle appeared in court on Friday

Witnesses "positively identified" Markle in two of the images believed to contain child sex abuse material.

In those images, the man pictured "appears to be in the age range of 30s to 40s", which suggests the alleged abuse dates back decades as Markle is currently 82 years old.

Last month, police in the Philippines obtained a warrant to examine devices allegedly removed from Markle's former home in the country.

Four boxes of material, including computers, phones, hard drives and old video tapes, were handed to officers by Meghan's half brother Thomas Markle Jr.

Markle Sr moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and lived in Cebu until May. He was then reportedly living in a Florida rehabilitation centre learning to walk again after having his leg amputated.

In October, London's Metropolitan Police confirmed that it had received a letter in 2019 making a "number of claims" relating to Markle Sr.

The Met said information contained in the letter, which related to allegations against Meghan's father outside the UK, was passed on to the office of the Duke and Duchess of Sussex, but it did not share it with other law enforcement agencies.

The force has since voluntarily referred itself to the Independent Office for Police Conduct (IPOC) over its handling of the letter.

In a statement released in October after the allegations against Markle Sr emerged, the Duchess of Sussex said: "I am deeply distressed at the news that has come to light in the last 24 hours, and my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

Meghan has been estranged from her father since 2018. The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year.

#43. Russian strikes wound nine and damage Ukraine's power network ahead of talks

Employees inspect the damage of a kindergarten in Kyiv following a Russian missile attack

Overnight drone and ballistic missile attacks damaged Ukrainian energy infrastructure, President Volodymyr Zelenskyhas said, causing blackouts and disrupting water supplies.

Zelensky said 12 Ukrainian regions came under attack with the capital Kyiv hit hardest.

Nine people are reported to have been hurt across the country, including one child, in the wave of attacks that consisted of more than 30 ballistic missiles and over 170 drones.

The attacks come before Ukrainian and Russian delegations are due to take part in trilateral talks in Abu Dhabi next week.

Zelensky wrote on Telegram, external that Moscow was preparing for the talks by launching attacks on Ukraine's "critical infrastructure".

Temperatures are dropping in Ukraine, and parts of the country have had the first winter snow.

"The situation in the capital is difficult, with disruptions to electricity, water, and heating supplies. Residential buildings, the North Bridge, and a kindergarten have been damaged," Zelensky said.

"In the Kyiv region, there were strikes on substations that connect the Rivne and Khmelnytsky nuclear power plants to Kyiv and central Ukraine."

In his latest update, Kyiv's Mayor Vitali Klitschko said the city's water supply system is back to operating normally. He also said around 60% of the city's apartment buildings were connected to the heating system.

The energy minister, Denys Shmyhal, said there were power cuts in five regions but did not say how many homes were affected.

Debris and a broken window inside the kindergarten in Kyiv

Zelensky added that beyond Kyiv, substations were damaged in Vinnytsia, Zhytomyr, Rivne, Kharkiv and Dnipro regions.

The latest attacks come after US First Lady Melania Trump hosted Russian and Ukrainian delegations at the White House on Thursday.

She called for a "more enduring" ceasefire in Ukraine following a truce last week in the southern region of Kherson.

She thanked both Zelensky and Russian President Vladimir Putin, making no reference to the origins of the war, which began when Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, which has caused thousands of civilian casualties and the displacement of millions.

Hundreds of residents of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky facing starvation and constant bombardment were able to evacuate during a six-day ceasefire, which both Moscow and Kyiv credited Trump with brokering.

"The lives of innocent people must transcend the division of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace," she said.

Earlier this week, a number of people were killed when glide bombs hit an aid centre near Zaporizhzhia, with the death toll rising to 55 as teams search deeper into the ruins.

Zelensky added in his Telegram post on Saturday following the latest attacks that Ukraine was counting on increased bilateral support from the United States.

He wrote: "We are counting on unity between both parties on issues of security, support for Ukraine, condemnation of Russian aggression, and increased sanctions pressure on Putin.

"The resolve and consistency of the United States in supporting Ukraine are of great importance in bringing peace closer."

Meanwhile, Zelenksy's spokesperson would not be drawn on reports that the Ukrainian president is to be invited to the UK for a state visit next year.

#39. Italian foreign minister says hackers leaked his personal data

Antonio Tajani says the hackers also targeted his family

Italy's Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani says he has been targeted by "cyber-criminals" who published his personal data online because of his country's support for Ukraine.

"For days now, cyber-criminals have been publishing my personal data on the dark web and seeking further information, effectively exposing me to new threats and intimidation," Tajani wrote on X.

He did not specify the nature of the threats, but said the hackers were also targeting his family.

The dark web is a hidden part of the internet that is not indexed by common search engines and can only be accessed with specialist software.

"Our position on Ukraine is the political reason I have been targeted," Tajani said.

"Defending the freedom of a sovereign state, taking a clear stand in support of a nation's democracy and independence, is dangerous in the eyes of these criminal entities."

Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni blamed the incident on pro-Russian hackers and offered Tajani her "full solidarity".

"The sale of confidential information about institutional representatives is a cause of serious concern and must be tackled with the utmost determination," she said in a statement.

Moscow has not publicly responded to the allegations.

Meloni also expressed concern about "circulation of false images" which she said were shared by members of Italy's opposition. She did not give details about the content of the images.

The Italian government has provided humanitarian and military support to Ukraine in its war against Russia.

Tajani, who is also the deputy prime minister, said the government was taking the threats seriously.

#38. Pope’s visit to Argentina sets up showdown with ‘hyper-capitalist’ Milei

President has opposing views on many issues, and once called Leo’s predecessor the ‘representative of the Evil One’

Trees are being planted in the garden. The brightly coloured buildings are getting another coat of paint. The choir rehearses. Hundreds of other residents, several of them using wheelchairs, practise their positions outside the sanctuary. Excitement is in the air, because on Monday 9 November, the Cottolengo Don Orione – a Catholic-run home for disabled people – will be the first stop on Pope Leo XIV’s first full day of his trip to Argentina.

But the pope’s visit to Claypole, about 26km from Buenos Aires, does not come at an especially happy time for the home, which counts about 400 residents with disabilities. Argentina’s president, Javier Milei, has made wide-ranging cuts to disability funding.

“[The government] cut off the supply of medications, special foods and diapers. They haven’t delivered any since last November. The matter is in court,” said the facility’s coordinator, Fernando Montero. The pope and Milei, he said, “have completely opposing ideologies”, arguing that the pope teaches that a person’s worth is not measured by whether they are productive, whereas Milei is a “hyper-capitalist” who believes only “those who compete the most should be rewarded”.

Pope Leo’s three days here – he began a three-country trip through South America in Uruguay on Friday, and will finish with six days in Peru – will be the first papal visit to Argentina since John Paul II in 1987. His predecessor, Pope Francis, who was Argentine, never returned during his papacy.

The trip could prove uncomfortable for Milei. A far-right libertarian economist who campaigned with a chainsaw as the symbol of his promised spending cuts, Milei called Francis an “imbecile” and “the representative of the Evil One on Earth” in 2020, and accused him of promoting communism, saying social justice was theft. Milei claims to have apologised when he met Francis in the Vatican in February 2024.

Since taking office the previous December, he has slashed public spending, which the government says was needed to curb inflation. The cost, however, was high. The thinktank Mate estimates that retirees on the minimum pension – 63% of the total – have lost about 30% of their purchasing power. Last Wednesday at the weekly retirees’ march outside Congress, a man held up a cardboard sign reading, “Save us Pope Leo XIV – Milei is killing us.”

Last month, Buenos Aires archbishop Jorge García Cuerva said Leo XIV would find “a divided Argentina and a large number of very poor brothers and sisters.”

But the differences between the pope and the president go beyond typical social issues. In May, Leo XIV called for “disarming” artificial intelligence, while Milei has said he would not regulate AI technology and has instead presented bills to build datacentres across the country and to grant legal status to “no-human corporations”. Federico Sturzenegger, a minister in charge of state cuts, says he has used AI to draft legislation.

Milagros Miceli, a sociologist and computer scientist who leads the Critical AI Lab in Berlin, said the “contradiction” between the two is “pretty evident”. She welcomed Leo’s visit, celebrating how the “powerful figure” of the pope intends to put “human beings at the centre by stripping these technologies of the values that are presented to us as taking precedence over humanity.”

Juan Grabois, a left-wing Peronist deputy and social leader who served in the Vatican’s Dicastery for Promoting Integral Human Development, said: “The idea of absolute deregulation is at the heart of the president’s agenda, and the replacement of the human element with these forms of algorithmic power has clearly also become a policy priority for the government.”

Disability funding has been a flashpoint between church and state. As Milei has clashed with Congress over a funding law that remains in limbo, Argentina’s conference of bishops wrote to the health minister, Mario Lugones, on 1 April, denouncing “the serious situation facing numerous institutions dedicated to the care and support of people with disabilities in our country”. The ministry has met the bishops twice since, in April and again on 5 October, but without resolving the impasse.

The government is trying to downplay potential tensions. “There’s no way the pope is going to come here to comment on local politics,” Sandra Pettovello, the minister overseeing social policy, said recently. Amnesty International, meanwhile, has written to the pope about Argentina, warning of its “concern about government decisions that affect the exercise of fundamental rights”.

All of this will be top of mind when Milei receives the pope at the airport on Sunday. From the disability home, the pope will go on to Buenos Aires and meet business and union leaders; then on Tuesday he will celebrate Mass at the grounds of FAdeA, the state-owned military aircraft factory in Córdoba, whose workers have protested against Milei’s cuts. Juan Cruz Esquivel, director of Buenos Aires University’s Barometer of Religions and Beliefs, says the itinerary speaks for itself.

“I don’t want to speculate [about what the pope will say], but clearly any mention of defending domestic production, labour, disability or social justice in the pope’s rhetoric naturally clashes with the ideology that Milei’s government has been promoting,” says Esquivel.

Mariano Oberlin, a Catholic priest in an impoverished neighbourhood of Córdoba, expects the clash to play out diplomatically. “Pope Leo has been saying it without confronting, but he keeps upholding the church’s social doctrine as the key axis of his ministry,” Oberlin said. “If Milei wants to use him, he’ll probably have to do a lot of juggling to make him say the opposite of what he’s been saying – and of what he’ll surely say when he comes: that the Gospel invites us to feel like brothers, to respect each person’s deep, inherent dignity, and to fight so that this dignity becomes real in access to rights.”

Since Francis’s tenure, social movements in Latin America have come closer to the Catholic church, and Argentina is no exception. Grabois, who was part of that process, sees the relationship as a two-way exchange. “[Francis] wasn’t the ‘good priest’ who came to help the poor,” he says, arguing that the exchanges helped Francis “develop his social thinking”. “Without the cartoneros (cardboard pickers) and the villeros (people from impoverished neighbourhoods), Francis would not have been Francis.”

To Grabois, the same is true for Leo. “Francis took notice of Leo because he saw him supporting the Peruvian indigenous movement.”

That doesn’t mean all Argentine social movements support the pope. On Saturday, a day before the visit, the LGBTQIA+ community will hold the 35th Pride March in Buenos Aires. María Rachid from the march’s organising committee said: “The church’s dogma regarding our families has not changed at any time, neither under the previous pope nor under this one”, though she notes that Francis’s words on gay rights “brought some relief to many families and their communities”.

But a new development is the rise of evangelical churches, while Catholic identification falls. Buenos Aires University found 57.7% of Argentines identify as Catholic and 17.4% as evangelical, compared with 76.5% and 9% nearly 20 years ago. Last week, Milei inaugurated an evangelical temple in Neuquén and gave a nakedly political speech promising to seek re-election next year. Although Catholicism remains Argentina’s majority religion, it has lost its cultural hegemony and its influence on legislative agenda, Esquivel said.

Nevertheless, a majority of Argentines expect a “message from the pope focused on the social and humanitarian agenda”. Grabois notes the transformative impact that first Francis and now Leo have had “with left-leaning activists” in Argentina. “I’m not speaking about atheists, but outright anticlerical people. People who, 10 years ago, held views that were very, very hostile toward the church,” he said. “I even see them as more fervent [about the visit] than typical churchgoers who attend Sunday Mass.” For Grabois, “in the face of dehumanisation, ordinary people are taking refuge in the only global voice that’s speaking out about this today – which is Francis and Leo’s.”

#34. Pope in 'pretty good health' as he starts Latin America tour

Pope Leo XIV has told reporters travelling with him to Latin America he is "in pretty good health", in his first comments on his condition since having surgery for lung cancer.

The Pope has arrived in Uruguay, the first stop of a 12-day tour which he is undertaking only three weeks after his operation.

The pontiff, 71, also said he was "very happy" to be able to make the trip which was thrown into doubt last month when doctors discovered a tumour during a routine scan.

The tour will cover Argentina and a much anticipated return to Peru, which the Vatican described as a "homecoming" as the Pope was based there for many years as a missionary, then a bishop.

"I must confess that, from the very first day, I never had any doubts; I was convinced that this journey had to go forward," the Pope told journalists on his plane to Uruguay.

"Thanks to the marvellous medical staff... I believe I am in quite good health," Pope Leo confirmed.

Last month's operation to remove a tumour was successful and his doctors say the cancer has not spread.

On Friday, he arrived in Monetevideo to a seafront lined with crowds shouting his name as his Popemobile passed.

In his first speech of the trip, the Pope said universal healthcare should be "accessible to everyone".

He told the crowd in Plaza Independencia the care he received made him think of the "many men and women who lack the means to obtain a timely diagnosis" or receive treatment.

He capped his day with a prayer at the Montevideo Cathedral.

Pope Leo will cover more than 20,000 miles on his sixth international tour.

On Sunday he will travel to Argentina, the homeland of his predecessor Pope Francis who did not return during his own papacy.

The Pope said his visit was about being near the people who were looking to the Church for a message of "hope".

#31. ANC faces anger over poor services as South Africans vote in local elections

Party expected to get less than a third of the vote amid frustration over water outages and power cuts

The African National Congress is on course for a bruising result in South Africa’s local elections on Wednesday as voters go to the polls amid deep discontent over water outages, power cuts and crumbling infrastructure.

The former liberation movement, which Nelson Mandela led to power in 1994 at the end of apartheid as South Africa’s first black president, is still likely to be the largest party. However, polls showed it getting less than a third of the vote, down from 45.6% at the last municipal elections in 2021 and 40.2% in 2024’s national elections.

In Kensington, a diverse Johannesburg suburb that has sustained repeated water and power outages in recent weeks, voters said they wanted to see change. The city is currently led by Dada Morero, the ANC mayor, in coalition with the far-left Economic Freedom Fighters (EFF) and the rightwing populist Patriotic Alliance (PA).

“Service delivery is bad, it’s horrible,” said Tshepiso Makotse, 26, a call centre worker. “Water, electricity – everything, basically.”

Makotse said she had split her two votes, casting one for the centre-right Democratic Alliance (DA) local councillor and her party vote for the leftwing uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) party of the former ANC president Jacob Zuma. “I just want change,” she said.

Terrence Pieters, a retired boilermaker, said: “The infrastructure in Johannesburg is shot.” He declined to say which party he had voted for, other than it was “one of the offshoots of the DA”.

“I would like to see things get better and great people in charge,” said the 72-year-old. “But … there’s no one party that’s going to take over here. It’s going to be a circus, it’s going to be a coalition.”

Vote fragmentation means the ANC could still end up leading many city coalition governments while losing control of others.

Unstable coalitions are already a feature of many of South Africa’s 257 municipalities, and analysts point to their high turnover as a major reason for poor service delivery.

Johannesburg, a city of 4.8 million people that has faced water shortages for more than a week, has had 10 mayors in as many years. The neighbouring municipality of Ekurhuleni has had three mayors since 2021. To the north, in Tshwane, which contains the capital, Pretoria, the mayoralty has rotated between parties four times since 2020. In October, more than 800 people contracted a gastrointestinal illness from contaminated water in Bronkhorstspruit, in east Tshwane.

The ANC’s national leadership is open to working with the DA in Gauteng, according to South African media, mirroring the government of national unity, where the two parties are the largest in the national coalition. However, local ANC leaders are reportedly not in favour of a DA tie-up. Currently, the ANC governs in various coalitions across the province’s three metros, including under an ActionSA mayor in Tshwane.

In Johannesburg, the DA’s Helen Zille has led a spirited campaign, with viral stunts including the 75-year-old snorkelling in a pothole and kayaking along a flooded street. However, the former Cape Town mayor is still viewed with suspicion by many, especially black voters, due to past comments appearing to excuse colonialism. Some voters also point to soaring housing costs in Cape Town, which has been run by the DA since 2006, as a reason not to vote for Zille.

The former Johannesburg DA mayor Herman Mashaba, who now leads ActionSA, which is polling around 10%, has ruled out working with Zille in Johannesburg. Mashaba has called Zille a “racist”, an allegation she has always fervently denied. The ANC, for its part, has three possible mayoral candidates for the city, amid factional infighting.

In eThekwini metro, which contains the port city of Durban, the MK party was polling above 40%. Zuma, its 84-year-old leader, who has weathered and denied numerous corruption allegations, is set to take many of the votes of his former party, which won 42% in eThekwini in 2021 and is now polling around 10%.

The DA looks set to retain its majority in Cape Town, although probably with less than the 58% of the vote it won in 2021, as unaffordable housing turns more voters against the party.

South Africa’s electoral commission will have a week to announce the final results of Wednesday’s vote, which chooses councillors and parties, with mayors not directly elected. Parties will then have two weeks from the announcement to form councils and elect mayors.

#29. Paraguay's former president charged after bank collapse

Late night political drama as PeÃ±a is impeached and arrested

Prosecutors in Paraguay have charged former president Santiago PeÃ±a over his alleged role in a banking scandal that led to his impeachment.

He has been accused of breach of trust and administration for personal gain.

PeÃ±a, who denies any wrongdoing, was forced from office after a bank collapse triggered a crisis that left more than half of the country's population without access to their accounts.

He was arrested on Friday minutes after lawmakers, including from his own party, unanimously voted to remove him from office following the collapse of Ueno Bank, which has links to his private business interests.

He had offered to step down but rather than accept his resignation, senators impeached PeÃ±a and charged him with misconduct, meaning he has been stripped of the immunity usually granted to ex-presidents.

PeÃ±a, who until last year held shares in the company that owned the bank, has denied any wrongdoing.

The impeachment charges that led to PeÃ±a's removal alleged he had conflicts of interest, manipulated regulatory oversight, diverted pension funds, and participated in an institutional cover-up involving the central bank.

PeÃ±a's deputy, Pedro Alliana, has been sworn in as the new interim president and promised not to "abandon" the millions of people affected by the scandal.

"Compatriots who are now going through a difficult time as a result of the financial collapse will be fully compensated," said Alliana, who will serve as president until 2028.

The demise of the digital bank left more than half of Paraguay's population without access to their savings after it was seized by Paraguay's central bank.

Long lines of people trying to withdraw money from Ueno branches snaked down the streets of the capital Asuncion on Tuesday and Wednesday.

An estimated three million depositors remain unable to access their savings and pensions, prompting public outrage particularly in the capital, AsunciÃ³n.

Protests erupted across the country in response, with thousands taking to the streets on Thursday to demand their money.

Earlier demonstrations featured clashes with the police, who used tear gas and water cannons on the crowd.

PeÃ±a's impeachment passed with 44 out of 45 senators voting in favour of it. One lawmaker was absent.

"It was never my intention to betray the trust you placed in me," PeÃ±a said in a video posted on X announcing his resignation, adding his priority was "the peace of mind of Paraguayans who entrusted their savings to our financial system".

Afterwards, senate president Basilio NÃºÃ±ez said PeÃ±a was found guilty of "dereliction of duty".

Pedro Alliana takes the oath of office as interim President

Senator Rafael Filizzola said: "We are facing the biggest corruption scandal in our democratic history â a tremendous blow to the state, to private citizens, and to the country's credibility and image."

Analysts say PeÃ±a is likely to now be prosecuted over the bank's collapse.

His defence lawyer Ricardo Preda described the allegations as a "reckless accusation" that was "irresponsible and opportunistic".

#25. UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

Michel Barnier says there will be 'no cherry-picking' in a deal for the UK to returns to the EU

The prime minister has said rejoining the bloc's internal market was among options he could pursue as part of a reshaping of ties since the UK's exit from the EU nearly six years ago.

But Michel Barnier told the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg that EU members would insist on the UK readopting certain conditions, such as the free movement of people, to gain access.

He said there was not a "single market Ã  la carte" menu from which the UK could pick and choose what elements it wanted to adopt.

Barnier - who led the EU delegation during Brexit talks between 2016 and 2021, and had a three-month spell as French prime minister in 2024 - said the "door is open" within Europe to the UK rejoining as an EU member.

But he insisted that any move to rejoin the single market - which allows for goods to be freely traded without border controls - would require the UK to follow the same terms as other members.

He added that this included compliance with regulations designed to promote the circulation of goods, services, capital and people, referred to collectively within EU institutions as the "four freedoms".

"There will be no possibility to have one freedom and not the other. The four freedoms are clearly the four conditions to be inside a single market."

Since becoming prime minister in July, Burnham has suggested he could commit Labour to pursuing a much closer future relationship with Europe, including asking to rejoin the organisation as a full member, though he has ruled out major changes before the next election.

His predecessors have struck a series of deals with the EU on different aspects of how the UK and EU interact post-Brexit.

Both sides are hoping to use a meeting in Brussels in 10 days' time to unveil deals on food standards, youth visas and re-linking British industry to the EU's carbon trading regime.

Why the prime minister wants to talk about Brexit again

Of the four freedoms, the free movement of people - which allows EU citizens to live and work elsewhere in the bloc without a visa - was a key issue during the 2016 Brexit referendum.

Burnham's immediate predecessor, Sir Keir Starmer, ruled out re-establishing it for the UK in Labour's 2024 election manifesto and any move to advocate it again would prove a key dividing line with the Conservatives and Reform UK at a future poll.

Burnham recently said in an interview that he would not re-establish free movement of people without a referendum, prompting Downing Street to clarify that he was not advocating its return at this point.

He has previously said he wanted to lay out "different options" for the relationship with Europe at the Brussels summit later this month, before trying to find a consensus with the British public on a new relationship.

Barnier also predicted that EU countries would reject reinstating the British financial rebate, first negotiated in 1985, that lowered UK contributions towards the EU's annual budget while it was a member.

The UK's decision to leave the bloc had created "a new situation", he said, meaning a new rebate would be "off the table".

Barnier also compared Reform leader Nigel Farage to French politician Marine Le Pen. He said: "We have our own Farage. It is Mrs Le Pen. The same populism, the same lies, the same false promises."

Asked by Kuenssberg to clarify what he was referring to with his accusation of lies, Barnier said "for instance, when Mr Farage and Mr Johnson promised the UK people to get back [Â£350] million per week, it was not true, it was not possible. It was a lie".

The Vote Leave campaign, of which Boris Johnson became a key figure, has been criticised previously for the slogan on the side of their campaign bus that said Britain sent Â£350m a week to the EU, adding: "Let's fund our NHS instead".

Johnson said in 2017 that "once we have settled our accounts, we will take back control of roughly Â£350m per week. It would be a fine thing as many of us have pointed out if a lot of that money went on the NHS".

Critics have said that the Â£350m per week was an approximate sum which represents a "gross contribution" and did not take into account the UK's Â£75m-a-week rebate nor the EU's investments in the UK.

Johnson has since defended the claim, saying the Â£350m was based on the UK's gross contribution to the EU, and was in fact an underestimate as UK contributions were due to rise.

âIn his memoirs published in 2024, Johnson said EU money spent in the UK was not "democratically controlled by the British people", and "even if the net figure was only Â£175m, that was still a hell of a lot of money".

Farage, who led Leave UK and was not part of the Vote Leave campaign so not connected to the Â£350m claim, said: "It is the same old Barnier - arrogant, out of touch and a bully. He vindicates our decision to leave every time he opens his mouth."

#21. British healthcare worker with Ebola evacuated from DR Congo to UK

Patient is ⁠in ​stable condition and will be transferred to a specialist ‘high consequence’ infectious disease unit

A British healthcare worker with Ebola is being evacuated from the Democratic Republic of the Congo to the UK for medical treatment, it has been confirmed.

“A British citizen currently in the DR Congo has tested positive for Ebola,” a spokesperson for the UK government said in a statement on Wednesday. “The UK has well-established procedures for dealing with cases like this, and those processes have been activated … The risk to the UK public from Ebola remains low.”

The patient, whose identity is unknown, is in a stable condition and is expected to arrive in the UK on Wednesday or Thursday before being transferred in a specialist ambulance to a “high consequence” infectious disease unit, according to an official document seen by Reuters.

A humanitarian organisation sent the worker to DR Congo to help in the response to the outbreak, which is the second largest on record. It is the first case involving a British national contracting Ebola in the current outbreak.

The Royal Free hospital in London has a high-level isolation unit for infectious diseases such as Ebola; other centres are in Newcastle and Liverpool.

Ebola is a highly contagious, severe and often fatal illness. It is spread through direct contact with body fluids such as the blood or vomit of infected people, or dead bodies.

The current outbreak, declared on 15 May by DR Congo’s health ministry, has surpassed 10,000 cases, and 5,000 deaths. It is the 17th in DR Congo since Ebola was identified, was caused by the Bundibugyo virus, and has become the largest and deadliest in the country’s history. The Bundibugyo virus has no approved treatment, though vaccine trials are under way.

Since the outbreak started, it has spread to Kenya and Uganda in east Africa, while last month Nigeria confirmed a case in the capital, Abuja, after a woman who arrived from Uganda tested positive for the virus. She, along with a close contact, were evacuated to Switzerland for treatment.

In an interview with the Guardian last month, Dr Jean Kaseya, the director general of the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), said the “unprecedented” spread of the Ebola outbreak meant it could reach the UK and other countries.

“We want all countries to know that they are not safe if they don’t take appropriate measures,” he said. “Each country, not only in the African region, but all continents, must now increase the preparedness level and the possibility for treatment.

“It is the first time three African regions have been affected [by Ebola]. The geographic extension of this outbreak is unprecedented.”

Other patients who have been medically evacuated for specialised care since the start of the outbreak include two sent to Germany and one to the Netherlands. Another case was reported in France in July.

British health authorities say the risk ⁠to the wider public from imported Ebola cases remains low and the NHS has procedures in place to manage suspected and confirmed infections.

There have only been four confirmed cases recorded in Britain since 1976, ‌according to the UK Health Security Agency.

#19. US offers $20m reward for information about Iran's nuclear programme

The US has announced a reward of up to $20 million for information about Iran's nuclear programme.

The State Department's Rewards for Justice is also offering to potentially relocate the person who provides such details.

President Donald Trump claimed last year that Iran's nuclear facilities had been "completely and totally obliterated" following a bombing campaign by the US and Israel.

Iran insists its nuclear programme is peaceful and that it has no intention of developing a nuclear weapon.

Meanwhile, the US military says it is setting up a task force to enhance Washington's efforts to restrict Iran's movement of weapons and sanctioned material across its borders.

The US military's Central Command (Centcom) said Task Force Iron Gate would operate from Tampa, Florida, and would work with other US government agencies.

As well as the movement of weapons, it will also focus on dual-use and illegal revenue streams.

Centcom estimates that its naval blockade of Iran's ports has prevented the country from exporting more than $25bn in crude oil.

Major construction ongoing near Iran missile bases, satellite images suggest

A US State Department statement about its multi-million dollar reward said it was offering the money for information about the Iranian programme, including "facilities, equipment and associated personnel".

It said Iran's "lack of compliance with the IAEA [the International Atomic Energy Agency] on safeguards obligations has led to the agency's loss of 'continuity of knowledge' regarding Iran's production and inventory of key components like [nuclear] centrifuges".

Centrifuges are needed to produce enriched uranium, which can be used to make reactor fuel for civilian use but also for nuclear weapons if enriched to around 90% purity or higher.

There had been mounting speculation that the US might resume significant strikes in Iran after the midterm elections, though Trump told reporters, prior to the polls, that he was in "no rush" to do so.

In an interview with Fox News on the eve of the midterms, the president also said the US "might need to go after Pickaxe [Mountain]", an underground site linked to Iran's nuclear programme.

This was "not a big deal" and could be done in "one hour", Trump said.

The president has repeatedly threatened to bomb the suspected underground site on previous occasions.

However, both Trump and Vice-President JD Vance have said public opposition to the war on Iran fuelled heavy losses for their Republican Party in this week's midterm elections, which has led to speculation the White House may no longer seek an escalation in the conflict.

On Friday, Trump insisted that Iran was keen to end the eight-month conflict with the US, stressing the damage to Tehran's economy and military.

"We're dealing with them now. They want to make a deal very badly," he said.

Qatar's foreign ministry spokesman said on Tuesday that both sides were "reviewing the ideas and proposals we have presented" and that it remained hopeful of a deal being agreed.

Iranian negotiators had discussed with mediators a proposal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz within two days once the US met its seven conditions.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.
